C O M M O N O R D E R
CHITRA VENKATARAMAN,J.
The batch of Writ Petitions before this Court are filed by th
members of different Co-operative Societies seeking a Writ o
Declaration to declare the provisions of the Tamil Nadu Co-operativ
Societies (Third Amendment) Ordinance 2012, as unconstitutional an
ultra vires the Constitution, particularly Part IXB of the Constitutio
of India. Subsequent to the filing of the Writ Petitions, th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Ordinance had become an Act and accordingly, the petitioners have file
petitions before this Court, amending the prayer to declare the amende
Act as unconstitutional. Accordingly, the amendment petitions filed i
the Writ Petitions are ordered. On notice, the State has filed
common counter in all these Writ Petitions.
2. It is a matter of record that approximately for about 1
years, there had been no election in the Co-operative Societies fo
some reason or other to elect the members of the Board o
Directors/Governing Body of the cooperative societies. In the wake o
the above-said fact, in the place of the Board/governing body, Specia
Officers were appointed by the Registrar for a specified period no
exceeding six months, to manage the affairs of the registered society
pending constitution of a new Board. The term of office of the Specia
Officer was extended from time to time. As of today, in the place o
the Board/governing body, we have only Special Officers managing th
affairs of the society. In this background, Section 89A was inserte
by the Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies (Amendment) Act of 2001 (Tami
Nadu Act 12 of 2001), effective from 12th June 2001. Through thi
provision, powers were conferred on the Special Officer appointed unde
Section 88 of the Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies Act, 1989 and th
Administrator appointed under Section 33 to admit any individua
eligible for admission as a member of the society. The validity of th
said provision came up for consideration before the Full Bench of thi
Court. The decision of the Full Bench is reported in 2006 (1) CTC
(K.Nithyanandam Vs. State of Tamil Nadu).
3. This Court struck down the provisions as violative of Articl
19(1)(c) and not saved by any of the grounds under Clause (4) o
Article 19. In so holding, this Court followed the decisions of th
Apex Court reported in (1997) 3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj V. M.L.Nagaraj
and (2000) 6 SCC 127 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Keral
V. T.A.Kuttappan). In the above-said decisions, the Apex Court hel
that enrolment of new members in the cooperative society by the Specia
Officers would be detrimental to the democratic process of the societ
and as the cooperative society is expected to function in a democrati
manner, such a power should be exercised by an elected committee only
Thus, following the decision of the Apex Court, this Court pointed ou
that the admission of new members and the examination with regard t
the eligibility to be the member of a society are entirely in th
discretion of the Board of Directors elected by the general body.
Special Officer or an Administrator, appointed temporarily to manag
the affairs of the society, cannot be allowed to alter the compositio
of the society by conferring power on the Special Officer for enrollin
new members. This Court further pointed out "the admission of member
is a primary right of the society. In a democratic set up, it is th
general body/ executive committee which would alone have the power t
admit new members who satisfy the required criteria as per the bye-law
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

of the society. The Special Officer cannot enter into any conflictin
policy decision by admitting new members without the consent of othe
members. The Special Officer is assigned with a duty to administer th
day-to-day administration for a limited period with a limited purpose
Thus holding, this Court declared Section 89A of the Act as arbitrary
unreasonable, ultra vires and unconstitutional, as it would b
detrimental to the democratic process and disturbance to the democrati
set up, affecting the democratic right of the general body of th
society, which would amount to a declaration by the Legislature makin
the early judicial declaration in the decision reported in AIR 2000 S
2378 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies V. T.A.Kuttappan
ineffective and invalid. Thus as of today, all the cooperativ
societies' affairs are managed only by the Special Officers and ther
are no new members inducted into the society on account of the absenc
of a governing body, who alone were empowered to admit a person as
member of the society as per the unamended Section 21(2) of the Act.
4. While matters stood thus, under the Constitution (97
Amendment) Act, 2011, Article 19(1)(c) was amended, making the right t
form an association or co-operative Society, a fundamental right
Article 43-B was inserted in Part IV - Directive Principles of Stat
Policy, that "the State shall endeavour to promote voluntary formation
autonomous functioning, democratic control and professional Managemen
of Co-operative Societies." Apart from this, a separate Chapter wa
inserted in the Constitution as Part IXB under the Head "the Co
operative Societies". Under Article 243ZH, apart from givin
definition to various terms such as, "Board", "Co-operative Society
"Registrar" etc., Article 243ZI enjoined on the States to enact law t
make provisions with respect to incorporation, regulation and windin
up of co-operative societies based on the principles of voluntar
formation, democratic member-control, member-economic participation an
autonomous functioning; Article 243ZJ - The number and term of member
of Board and its office bearers; Article 243ZK - Election of members o
Board; Article 243ZL - Supersession and suspension of Board and interi
management and Article 243ZN - Convening of general body meetings etc
about which we shall deal later.
5. The amended Articles 243ZK and 243ZL emphasize the need fo
holding elections to elect members of the board before the expiry o
the term of the Board, so that there is continuity in th
administration of the society, that the board shall not be suspended o
kept under suspension for a period exceeding six months. In the wake o
these constitutional provisions through Ordinance No.14 of 2012 date
3.8.2012, the State legislature amended Section 21(2) Clause (i) of th
Act, that in the absence of the Board, the general body will have th
authority to admit new members. The provisos under the sub section ar
also accordingly amended The explanatory statement on the amendmen
made reads as under:
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

" EXPLANATORY STATEMENT.
Under the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act,
1983 (Tamil Nadu Act 30 of 1983) the Board of
Directors of a registered society is vested with
the power to admit, any individual eligible for
admission, as a member of the society. At present
Special Officers are managing the affairs of the
registered society. When the Special Officers were
empowered, by making specific provision in the said
Tamil Nadu Act 30 of 1983, to admit any individual
eligible for admission, as a member of the Co-operative Society, the full Bench of the High Court
of Madras in K. Nithyanandam Vs. State of Tamil
Nadu and Others (2006-1-L.W.363) declared the said
provision as arbitrary, unreasonable, ultravires
and unconstitutional, by observing as follows:—
“In view of the above discussion, this Court is of
the view that the Co-operative Societies are
constituted to function through the democratic
management by the General Body through its elected
board which alone is empowered to enroll the new
members.”.
In view of the above, new members could not be
admitted in the Co-operative Societies for the past
11 years.
2.Article 43-B in Part IX-B of the Constitution
envisages that the State shall endeavour and
promote voluntary formation, autonomous
functioning, democratic control and professional
Management of Co-operative Societies. In order to
pave the way for democratic functioning of the Co-operative Societies in tune with the provisions of
the Constitution, the Government have considered to
admit new members in the Co-operative Societies by
empowering the General Body of the Co-operative
Societies to admit, any individual eligible for
admission, as a member of the Society and have
decided to amend the said Tamil Nadu Act 30 of 1983
suitably for the said purpose.
3.The Ordinance seeks to give effect to the above
decision."
6. On 16.11.2012, the amendment made through the ordinance wa
carried through under the Amending Act under the Tamil Nadu Co
operative Societies (Third Amendment) Act, 2012 (Act 37 of 2012
Section 21(2)(i) was amended, whereby, the expression "or by th
general body where there is no board" after the expression "by th
board" was inserted. Corresponding amendments were also brought in t
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

the two provisos to the said Sub-section. Thus the petitioners who ar
all members in different co-operative societies challenge the validit
of the amendment made to Section 21(2)(i) of the Act.
7. The amended provisions in Constitution 97th Amendment Act i
Article 19(1)(c), Article 43-B, Part -IX-B and amended Section 21(2) o
the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act are as follows:
" 19. Protection of certain rights regarding freedom
of speech, etc. - (1) All citizens shall have the
right -
(a) to freedom of speech and expression;
(b) to assemble peaceably and without arms;
(c) to form associations or unions or co-operative
societies;
(d) to move freely throughout the territory of
India;
..............
43-B. Promotion of co-operative societies - The
State shall endeavour to promote voluntary
formation, autonomous functioning, democratic
control and professional management of co-operative
societies.
PART IXB
THE CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETIES
243ZH. Definitions.-- In this Part, unless the context
otherwise requires,--
(a) "authorised person" means a person referred to as
such in article 243ZQ;
(b) board" means the board of directors or the governing
body of a co-operative society, by whatever name called,
to which the direction and control of the management of
the affairs of a society is entrusted to;
(c) "co-operative society" means a society registered or
deemed to be registered under any law relating to co-operative societies for the time being in force in any
State;
(d) "multi-State co-operative society" means a society
with objects not confined to one State and registered or
deemed to be registered under any law for the time
being in force relating to such co-operatives;
(e) "officer bearer" means a President, Vice-President,
Chairperson, Vice-Chairperson, Secretary or Treasurer of
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

a co-operative society and includes any other person to
be elected by the board of any co-operative society;
(f) Registrar" means the Central Registrar appointed by
the Central Government in relation to the multi-State co-operative societies and the Registrar for co-operative
societies appointed by the State Government under the law
made by the Legislature of a State in relation to co-operative societies;
(g) "State Act" means any law made by the Legislature of
a State;
(h) "State level co-operative society" means a co-operative society having its. area of operation extending
to the whole of a State and defined as such in any law
made by the Legislature of a State.
243ZI. Incorporation of co-operative societies.--Subject
to the provisions of this Part, the Legislature of a
State may, by law, make provisions with respect to the
incorporation, regulation and winding up of co-operative
societies based on the principles of voluntary formation,
democratic member-control, member-economic participation
and autonomous functioning.
243ZJ. Number and term of members of board and its office
bearers.--
(1) The board shall consist of such number of directors
as may be provided by the Legislature of a State, by law:
Provided that the maximum number of directors of a co-operative society shall not exceed twenty-one:
Provided further that the Legislature of a State shall,
by law, provide for the reservation of one seat for the
Scheduled Castes or the Scheduled Tribes and two seats
for women on board of every co-operative society
consisting of individuals as members and having members
from such class or category of persons.
(2) The term of office of elected members of the board
and its office bearers shall be five years from the date
of election and the term of office bearers shall be
conterminous with the term of the board:
Provided that the board may fill a casual vacancy on the
board by nomination out of the same class of members in
respect of which the casual vacancy has arisen, if the
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

term of office of the board is less than half of its
original term.
(3) The Legislature of a State shall, by law, make
provisions for co-option of persons to be members of the
board having experience in the field of banking,
management, finance or specialisation in any other field
relating to the objects and activities undertaken by the
co-operative society, as members of the board of such
society:
Provided that the number of such co-opted members shall
not exceed two in addition to twenty-one directors
specified in the first proviso to clause (1):
Provided further that such co-opted members shall not
have the right to vote in any election of the co-operative society in their capacity as such member or to
be eligible to be elected as office bearers of the board:
Provided also that the functional directors of a co-operative society shall also be the members of the board
and such members shall be excluded for the purpose of
counting the total number of directors specified in the
first proviso to clause (1).
243ZK. Election of members of board.--(1) Notwithstanding
anything contained in any law made by the Legislature of
a State, the election of a board shall be conducted
before the expiry of the term of the board so as to
ensure that the newly elected members of the board assume
office immediately on the expiry of the office of members
of the outgoing board.
(2) The superintendence, direction and control of the
preparation of electoral rolls for, and the conduct of,
all elections to a co-operative society shall vest in
such an authority or body, as may be provided by the
Legislature of a State, by law:
Provided that the Legislature of a State may, by law,
provide for the procedure and guidelines for the conduct
of such elections.
243ZL. Supersession and suspension of board and interim
management.--(1) Notwithstanding anything contained in
any law for the time being in force, no board shall be
superseded or kept under suspension for a period
exceeding six months:
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Provided that the board may be superseded or kept under
suspension in case--
(i) of its persistent default; or
(ii) of negligence in the performance of its duties; or
(iii) the board has committed any act prejudicial to the
interests of the co-operative society or its members; or
(iv) there is stalemate in the constitution or functions
of the board; or
(v) the authority or body as provided by the Legislature
of a State, by law, under clause (2) of article 243ZK.,
has failed to conduct elections in accordance with the
provisions of the State Act:
Provided further that the board of any such co-operative
society shall not be superseded or kept under suspension
where there is no Government shareholding or loan or
financial assistance or any guarantee by the Government:
Provided also that in case of a co-operative society
carrying on the business of banking, the provisions of
the Banking Regulation Act, 1949(10 of 1949) shall also
apply:
Provided also that in case of a co-operative society,
other than a multi-State co-operative society, carrying
on the business of banking the provisions of this clause
shall have the effect as if for the words "six months",
the words "one year" had been substituted.
(2) In case of supersession of a board, the administrator
appointed to manage the affairs of such co-operative
society shall arrange for conduct of elections within the
period specified in clause (1) and handover the
management to the elected board.
(3) The Legislature of a State may, by law, make
provisions for the conditions of service of the
administrator.
243ZM. Audit of accounts of co-operative societies.--(1)
The Legislature of a Stale may, by law, make provisions
with respect to the maintenance of accounts by the co-operative societies and the auditing of such accounts at
least once in each financial year.
(2) The Legislature of a State shall, by law, lay down
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

the minimum qualifications and experience of auditors and
auditing firms that shall be eligible for auditing
accounts of the co-operative societies.
(3) Every co-operative society shall cause to be audited
by an auditor or auditing firms referred to in clause (2)
appointed by the general body of the cooperative society:
Provided that such auditors or auditing firms shall be
appointed from a panel approved by a State Government or
an authority authorised by the State Government in this
behalf.
(4) The accounts of every co-operative society shall be
audited within six months of the close of the financial
year to which such accounts relate.
(5) The audit report of the accounts of an apex co-operative society, as may be defined by the State Act,
shall be laid before the State Legislature in the manner,
as may be provided by the State Legislature, by law.
243ZN. Convening of general body meetings.--The
Legislature of a State may, by law, make provisions that
the annual general body meeting of every co-operative
society shall be convened within a period of six months
of close of the financial year to transact the business
as may be provided in such law.
243ZO. Right of a member to get information.--(1) The
Legislature of a State may, by law, provide for access to
every member of a co-operative society to the books,
information and accounts of the co-operative society kept
in regular transaction of its business with such member.
(2) The Legislature of a State may, by law, make
provisions to ensure the participation of members in the
management of the co-operative society providing minimum
requirement of attending meetings by the members and
utilising the minimum level of services as may be
provided in such law.
(3) The Legislature of a State may, by law, provide for
co-operative education and training for its members.
243ZP. Returns.-- Every co-operative society shall file
returns, within six months of the close of every
financial year, to the authority designated by the State
Government including the following matters, namely:--
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

(a) annual report of its activities;
(b) its audited statement of accounts;
(c) plan for surplus disposal as approved by the general
body of the co-operative society;
(d) list of amendments to the bye-laws of the co-operative society, if any;
(e) declaration regarding date of holding of its general
body meeting and conduct of elections when due; and
(f) any other information required by the Registrar in
pursuance of any of the provisions of the State Act.
243ZQ. Offences and penalties.--(1) The Legislature of a
State may, by law, make provisions for the offences
relating to the co-operative societies and penalties for
such offences.
(2) A law made by the Legislature of a State under clause
(1) shall include the commission of the following act or
omission as offences, namely:--
(a) a co-operative society or an officer or member
thereof wilfully makes a false return or furnishes false
information, or any person wilfully not furnishes any
information required from him by a person authorised in
this behalf under the provisions of the State Act;
(b) any person wilfully or without any reasonable excuse
disobeys any summons, requisition or lawful written order
issued under the provisions of the State Act;
(c) any employer who, without sufficient cause, fails to
pay to a cooperative society amount deducted by him from
its employee within a period of fourteen days from the
date on which such deduction is made;
(d) any officer or custodian who wilfully fails to
handover custody of books, accounts, documents, records,
cash, security and other property belonging to a co-operative society of which he is an officer or custodian,
to an authorised person; and
(e) whoever, before, during or after the election of
members of the board or office bearers, adopts any
corrupt practice.
243ZR. Application to multi-State cooperative
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

societies.--The provisions of this Part shall apply to
the multi-State co-operative societies subject to the
modification that any reference to "Legislature of a
State", "State Act" or "State Government" shall be
construed as a reference to "Parliament", "Central Act"
or "the Central Government" respectively.
243ZS. Application to Union territories.--
The provisions of this Part shall apply to the Union
territories and shall, in their application to a Union
territory, having no Legislative Assembly as if the
references to the Legislature of a State were a reference
to the administrator thereof appointed under article 239
and, in relation to a Union territory having a
Legislative Assembly, to that Legislative Assembly:
Provided that the President may, by notification in the
Official Gazette, direct that the provisions of this Part
shall not apply to any Union territory or part thereof as
he may specify in the notification.
243ZT. Continuance of existing laws.--Notwithstanding
anything in this Part, any provision of any law relating
to co-operative societies in force in a State immediately
before the commencement of the Constitution (Ninety-seventh Amendment) Act, 2011, which is inconsistent with
the provisions of this Part, shall continue to be in
force until amended or repealed by a competent
Legislature or other competent authority or until the
expiration of one year from such commencement, whichever
is less.
The Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act
21 - Qualifications for membership of society
(1)(a) Subject to the provisions of Section 23 --
(i) any individual competent to contract under Section 11
of the Indian Contract Act, 1872, (Central Act IX of
1872),
(ii) any other registered society,
(iii) the Government, and
(iv) any body of persons whether incorporated or not and
whether or not established by or under any law, if such
body is approved by the Government in this behalf by
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

general or special order, shall be eligible for admission
as a member of a registered society :
Provided that the individual or the registered society or
the body of persons referred to in items (i), (ii) and
(iv) shall possess such further qualifications as may be
specified in the Rules or the bye-laws:
Provided further that a Hindu undivided family as such
shall not be eligible for admission as a member of a
registered society:
Provided also that persons who are minors or of unsound
mind may be admitted as members of such class of
registered societies as may be prescribed and such
members shall possess only such privileges and rights of
members and be subject only to such liabilities of
members as may be prescribed:
Provided also that no individual shall be eligible for
admission as a member of any financing bank or apex
society, except as an associate member.
(b) Notwithstanding anything contained in this Act or in
any other law for the time being in force, every
individual member other than an associate member of every
financing bank and every apex society shall cease to be a
member of such bank or society, as the case may be, on
and from such date as the Government may, by
notification, specify and such individual member shall be
entitled to receive his share or interest in the capital
and other moneys due to him in such manner and within
such time as may be prescribed.
(2) (i) In the case of every registered society, every
individual eligible for admission as a member of any such
society under the provisions of this Act, the Rules and
the bye-laws of the society shall, on application made in
such form and in such manner as maybe prescribed, be
admitted by the board or by the general body where there
is no board as a member of the society with effect from
the date of receipt of such application in the office of
such society:
Provided that the board or the general body, as the case
may be, may, for good and sufficient reasons to be
recorded in the minutes of the meeting at which the
application for admission is considered, refuse admission
to any individual and the decision of the board or the
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

general body, as the case may be, shall be communicated
to the individual:
Provided further that if the decision of the board or the
general body, as the case may be, on the application is
not communicated to the individual within a period of
sixty days from the date of receipt of the application in
the office of the society, the individual shall be deemed
to have been admitted as a member of such society, on the
sixtieth day after the date of receipt of the application
in the office of the society.
(ii) Notwithstanding anything contained in class (i), or
in any other provision of this Act, the Registrar may,
either suo motu or on application at any time, by order
and after recording the reasons in writing, remove any
individual deemed to have been admitted as a member of
the society under clause (i) from such membership if such
individual is not eligible to be a member of such society
under the provisions of this Act, the Rules and the bye-laws of the society:
Provided that an order under this clause shall be passed
within such period as may be prescribed.
(iii) No order under clause (ii) shall be passed without
giving a reasonable opportunity of being heard to the
parties concerned."
8. Before going into the various contentions raised in the Wri
Petitions, we need to look at the broad scheme of the Act. The subjec
matter of legislation on Co-operative Society falls under Entry 32
List II of Seventh Schedule to the Constitution - "incorporation
"regulation" and "Winding up" of Corporations other than thos
specified in List I and universities unincorporated trading literar
scientific religious and other societies and associations; Co-operativ
Societies. Entry 41 List I of the Seventh Schedule lists th
incorporation, regulation and winding up of trading corporations
including banking, insurance and financial corporations, but no
including Co-operative Societies as a subject of legislation by th
union. Thus, by virtue of Entry 32 List II, the Tamil Nadu Co
operative Societies Act, 1983, (hereinafter referred to as the Act) wa
enacted by the State. The Act seeks to provide for an orderl
development of the co-operative movement in accordance with the co
operative principles for the promotion of thrift, self-help and mutua
aid among persons with common socio-economic needs so as to bring abou
improvement in agriculture and industry, better methods of productio
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

etc. The Act thus covers a wide range of co-operative societies, suc
as Joint Farming Societies, Co-operative Sugar Mills, Agricultural an
Rural Development Banks, Lift Irrigation Societies, Agricultura
Producers Marketing Societies, Consumer Societies, Credit Societie
other than Financing Banks and Employees Credit Societies; Dair
Societies; Farming Societies; Fisheries Societies; Industria
Societies; Irrigation Societies; Oil-Seeds Growers Societies
Processing Societies, Housing Societies, Banking societies, Weaver
Societies etc. Separate chapters are there in the Act to exclusivel
deal with Joint Farming Societies (Chapter XIII); Lift Irrigatio
Societies (Chapter XIV); Agricultural and Rural Development Ban
(Chapter XV) and Co-operative Sugar Mills (Chapter XVIII).
9. Section 2 of the Act defines the terms such as 'Board'
Section 2(7), 'byelaws' - Section 2(8) 'member' – Section 2(16) etc
Section 4 of the Act deals with Societies, which may be registered. I
says that subject to the provisions of this Act, a Society having th
object of promotion of the economic interests or general welfare of it
Members in accordance with Co-operative principles, or a societ
established with the object of facilitating the operations of such
Society, may be registered under this Act with limited or unlimite
liability. Every Society constituted must apply for registratio
before the Registrar in the prescribed form. Section 8 is the relevan
provision on the procedure for filing an application for registratio
before the Registrar; Section 9 deals with registration of the Societ
by the Registrar. As per the said Section, the application fo
registration has to be accompanied by a copy of the proposed bye law
of the Society; on satisfaction that the proposed bye laws of th
Society are not contrary to the provisions of the Act or Rules or t
the Co-operative principles or to any other law applicable to th
Society, the Registrar may register the Society and its bye laws. I
is open to the Registrar to refuse registration of the Society and it
bye laws. If the Registrar does not communicate the order of refusa
within a period of 120 days, it shall be deemed that the Registrar ha
registered the Society and its bye laws.
10. Chapter II, Rule 6 of the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societie
Rules, 1988 (hereinafter referred to as the 'Rules) deals wit
registration of co-operative Societies and its bye laws. It specifie
as many as 42 items as subject matter of byelaws. Thus a reading o
Rule 6 listing the subject matter of byelaws, shows among variou
things, qualification for admission to membership (Rule 6(1)(e)
admission of persons who are minors or of unsound mind and if so, th
privileges, rights and liabilities of such Members as specified in th
Rules 6(1)(g); admission of associate member, qualification therefo
(Rule 6(1)(h)); the nature and amount of share capital, if any of th
Society (Rule 6(1)(i)), entrance fee to be collected from the membe
(Rule 6(1)(j)), withdrawal of membership and the procedure to b
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

followed in cases of withdrawal, ineligibility and death of member
Rule 6(1)(k); the maximum share capital that can be refunded in any Co
operative year (Rule 6(1)(l)), the procedure for expulsion of Member
and as to how the assets and liabilities of an expelled Member in th
Society shall be dealt with Rule 6(1)(m); the constitution o
representative General Body and the powers of the General Body that ar
exercisable by the representative general body and the restrictions an
conditions subject to which the representative General Body ma
exercise its powers Rule 6(1)(n); the constitution of the Board and th
duties and powers of the Board and officers (Rule 6(1)(p)); convenin
of the meetings of the General Body and the Board, notice, business an
quorum for such meetings, conditions for, and manner of, adjournment o
such meetings (Rule 6(1)(w)); authorisation of the Officers to sig
documents/ contracts (Rule 6(1)(y)), Preparation and Submission o
Annual Accounts (Rule 6(1)(aa)), settlement of disputes (Rule 6(1
(ee)), inspection of accounts (Rule 6(1)(gg)), etc. Sub-Rule (2
states "The bye laws may also deal with such other matters incidenta
to the organisation and working of the Society and the management o
its business as may be deemed necessary."
11. Rule 9 deals with the procedure regarding amendment of bye
laws. According to the said Rule, every amendment of the bye-law
shall be made only by a resolution passed by a majority of the Member
present and voting at a meeting of the General Body, for which notic
of amendment of bye-laws has been given in accordance with the Rule an
the byelaws.
12. According to Section 11 of the Act, no amendment of any Bye
law of a registered Society shall be valid until the same has bee
registered under the Act. Under Sub-section (3), if the Registrar i
satisfied that an amendment of the byelaws is not contrary to th
provisions of this Act or the Rules or to the Co-operative principle
or to any other law applicable to the Society, he may register th
amendment. Sub-section (4) reserves the right to the Registrar t
refuse to register the amendment; but he has to communicate the sam
with the reasons for the refusal. On registration of an amendment t
the byelaws, he has to issue to the registered Society, a copy of th
amendment to the byelaws certified by him, which shall be a conclusiv
evidence that the amendment has been duly registered. Sub-section (9
states that an amendment of a Bye-law of the Society involves materia
change in the objects or operations of the Society; the amendment shal
be registered only subject to such Rules as may be made in this behalf
13. Section 12 prescribes the power of the Registrar to direc
amendment of byelaws. It says that where the Registrar is satisfie
that for the purpose of altering the area of operation of a registere
Society or for the purpose of improving the services rendered by it o
for any other purpose specified in the Rules, an amendment to th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

byelaws is necessary, after consulting the Board of the Financing Ban
in the manner prescribed, to which the Society is affiliated, call upo
the Society to show cause why the amendment should not be made. Rul
11 prescribes the purposes for which the Registrar may direct th
amendment of Bye-laws.
14. Chapter III of the Act prescribes the qualification fo
membership in the Society and their rights and liabilities. Section 2
of the Act lists out the qualification for membership in the Co
operative Society. Subject to the disqualification prescribed unde
Section 23, Sub-section (1) of Section 21 of the Act lists who ma
become a member of the Society. The proviso to the sub section state
that the persons referred to above shall possess such furthe
qualifications as may be specified in the Rules and the bye laws. Sub
section (2) of Section 21 of the Act deals with admission of members t
Co-operative Society. Sub-section (2), as it originally stood prior t
the amendment in 2012, which is under challenge before this Court now
states that every individual, eligible for admission as a member of an
registered society under the provisions of the Act, Rules and th
byelaws of the Society, shall be admitted by the Board as a Member o
the Society with effect from the date of receipt of such application i
the office of such Society. Proviso to Sub-section (2) gives th
authority to the Board to refuse admission of any individual. It als
prescribes the time limit within which the Board has to exercise it
authority as regards the admission of Members. Second Proviso to Sub
section (2) states that if the decision of the Board on the admissio
of a Member is not communicated to the individual within a period of 6
days from the date of receipt of the application in the office of th
Society, the individual shall be deemed to have been admitted as Membe
of such Society on the sixtieth day after the date of receipt of th
application in the office of the Society. Sub-Clause (ii) of Sub
section (2) to Section 21 of the Act reserves the right of th
Registrar, suo motu or on application, to remove any individua
admitted as Member of the Society under Sub-clause (i), from suc
Membership, if such individual is not eligible to be a Member of
Society under the provisions of the Act, Rules and byelaws of th
Society. After the amendment now introduced in the year 2012, unde
Sub-section 2(i) of Section 21 of the Act, wherever the Board is no
there, the General Body is now empowered to admit new member. Th
provisos also stand accordingly amended, empowering the General Body t
act in the absence of the Board.
15. Section 23 of the Act speaks about the disqualification o
Membership in the Society on grounds of insolvency, punishment fo
offences involving moral turpitude, expulsion from Membership under th
Act or that the Member did not possess the qualification wit
reference to the principal object of the Society prescribed in th
Rules or the byelaws of the Society. Sub-section (2) lists out th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

grounds under which a particular individual would cease to be a Membe
of the Society. As per Section 25 of the Act, a member of a registere
Society could be expelled, upon a resolution of a General Body passe
at a special meeting convened for the purpose by the votes of not les
than two-third of the total number of Members present and voting at th
meeting. Section 28 provides for restriction on transfer of share o
interest; Section 29 provides for restrictions on withdrawal of shar
or interest by members of registered societies and Section 30 provide
for transfer of share or interest on death of Member.
16. Chapter IV is concerned about the Management of the Registere
Societies. Section 32 of the Act deals with the authority of th
General Body as regards the Management of the Registered Society, whic
reads as follows:
"32. General Meetings.- (1)(a) Subject to the
provisions of this Act, the Rules and the byelaws, the
ultimate authority of a registered Society shall vest in
the General Body of its Members:
Provided that nothing contained in this clause shall
affect the exercise by the Board or any officer of a
registered Society of any power conferred on such Board
or such officer by this Act or the Rules or the byelaws."
Sub-section (2) of Section 32 of the Act speaks about the convening o
the General Meeting of a registered Society for the purposes of
"(a) approval of the budget for the ensuing year
with reference to the programme of the activities of
the Society prepared by the Board;
.......
(b) consideration of the audit report and the annual
report;
(c) disposal of the balance of the net profits as
specified in sub-section (2) of Section 72;
(d) consideration of the details of the services,
if any, rendered to any Member of the Board or any such
near relation as may be prescribed, of any Member of the
Board during the preceding year;
(dd) appraisal of the programme of the activities
of the Society; and
(e) consideration of any other matter which may be
brought forward in accordance with the byelaws;"
The proviso states that it is open to the registered Society to conven
general meetings as often as may be necessary in the interest of th
Society.
17. Sub-section (3) of Section 32 of the Act deals with the Boar
calling for a special general meeting of a registered Society and Sub
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

section (4) deals with the power of the Registrar to call for a meetin
under the stated circumstances of the Board's failure or refusal t
call for a meeting.
18. Section 33 of the Act speaks about the constitution and th
meetings of the Board. A reading of the provision under Section 33 o
the Act shows that the Management of the registered Society shall ves
in a Board constituted in accordance with the provisions of the Act
Rules and byelaws; that the Board shall exercise such powers an
perform such duties as may be conferred or imposed on it by the Act
the Rules and the byelaws. The Section also prescribes the constitutio
of the Board giving representation to the Scheduled Tribes an
Scheduled Castes and to the representatives of women. It also provide
for co-opting member/members, who is an expert or a person speciall
skilled in or having special knowledge of experience in matter
connected with the business or objects of the scheduled Cooperativ
Society.
19. Chapter V of the Act deals with duties and privileges of th
registered Societies. Section 39 of the Act states that th
registration of the Society shall render it a Body Corporate by th
name under which it was registered, with a power to hold property, t
enter into contracts, to institute and defend suits and other lega
proceedings and to do all things necessary for the purposes for whic
it was constituted. Chapter XV of the Act provides for winding up an
cancellation of registration of the registered Societies. Section 13
deals with winding up of the registered societies and Section 138 deal
with the appointment of a liquidator. Chapter XVIII of the Act deal
with penal provisions. Section 90(7) of the Act excludes th
applicability of the Arbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996; Section
(8) of the Act excludes the applicability of Section 34 of the Civi
Procedure Code. Section 175 of the Act states about the non
applicability of the Companies Act, 1956. Section 88 deals wit
circumstances under which the Board may be superseded by an order o
the Registrar and the appointment of a Special Officer to manage th
affairs of the society for a specified period not exceeding one yea
and the responsibilities and the duties of the Special Officer tha
subject to the control of the Registrar, he shall have the powers t
exercise all the powers of the Board and to such action as may b
required in the interests of the society. Rules 105 and 106 are th
related Rules in regard to the above provision. Section 89 is also a
regards the appointment of the Special Officer under state
circumstances.
20. Thus a reading of the provisions of the Act shows th
following powers vested on the General Body under various Sections:
(1) Division of the society into two or more societies (Section 1
(1)(a) of the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act, 1983).
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

(2) Amalgamation of two or more Societies (Section 13(2)(a) of th
Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act, 1983).
(3) Joint business with any other registered society o
undertaking of the Government (Section 18 of the Tamil Nadu Co
operative Societies Act, 1983).
(4) Expulsion of a member (Section 25(1) of the Tamil Nadu Co
operative Societies Act, 1983).
(5) Approval of Budget (Section 32(2)(a) of the Tamil Nadu Co
operative Societies Act, 1983).
(6) Considerations of Audit report and Annual report (Section 3
(2)(b) of the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act, 1983).
(7) Disposal of the balance of the net profits as specified in su
section (2) of Section 72 (Section 32(2)9c) of the Tamil Nadu Co
operative Societies Act, 1983).
(8) Consideration of the details of the services, if any, rendere
to any member of the board or any such near relation as may b
prescribed of any member of the board during the preceding yea
(Section 32(2)(d) of the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act, 1983).
(9) Appraisal of the programme of activities of the Societ
(Section 32(2)(e) of the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act, 1983).
(10) Passing resolution for winding up of the society (Section 13
(1) of the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act, 1983).
(11) writing off non recoverable assets (Section 178 of the Tami
Nadu Co-operative Societies Act, 1983).
(12) Amendment of byelaws (Rule 9(1) of the Tamil Nadu Co
operative Societies Rules, 1988).
(13) Conversion of the Society into a different class or categor
(Rule 19(2) of the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Rules, 1988).
(14) Removal of elected members of the board (Rule 61(1) of th
Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Rules, 1988).
(15) According prior approval for purchase of Vehicle (Rule 88(1
(a) of the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Rules, 1988).
21. As already seen, the byelaws of the Society have to provid
for various matters which are enumerated in Rule 6 of the Rules, apar
from what are given under the Act and the Rules. The byelaws have to b
in conformity with the provisions of the Act and the Rules, and abov
all, serves the objects of the cooperative movement fully.
22. Since the arguments of the petitioners in all these Wri
Petitions revolved on the importance of the byelaws governing th
affairs of the Society, particularly as regards the admission of ne
members by the Board and that the amendment cannot interfere with th
bye-laws empowering the Board to admit new members, the leadin
judgment of the Apex Court on the above topics needs to be seen, t
find out the impact of the amendment in Section 21(2) of the Act on th
Bye-laws of the Society, providing for admission to the new members b
the Board.
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

23. It may be noted herein that under Section 21(2) of the Act, a
it stood prior to the impugned amending Act, admission of new Membe
was to be done only by the Board. The present amendment seeks t
include the General Body, wherever there is no Board, for the purpose
of admission of new Members. Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution o
India was amended under Constitution 97
th Amendment Act, 2011, that th
right to form co-operative societies is now a fundamental righ
guaranteed under the Constitution of India. It is a well settle
principle that a Co-operative Society is a Society formed by persons
who voluntarily associate together for the promotion of their economi
interests. Voluntariness is thus considered to be the cardina
principle of co-operation and no one can be compelled to join in o
prevent one from withdrawing from the society except in the manne
provided for under the Act and the bye laws of the Society
Recognising the concept of voluntariness, Article 19(1)(c) of th
Constitution of India has made this right to form a Co-operativ
Society, a fundamental right.
24. Touching on the laudable object on the forming o
Co-operative Society, in the decision reported in AIR 1998 SC 241
(Myurdhwaj Co-operative Group Housing Society Ltd. V. Presidin
Officer, Delhi Co-operative Tribunal), the Apex Court pointed out tha
a Co-operative Society is formed with the laudable object to inculcat
a spirit to work in a group, ready for rendering benefits to th
Members through Co-operative contributions. The Apex Court pointed ou
that the Society can lay down its policy to deal with differen
situations, as may arise from time to time. In the Full Bench decisio
of this Court reported in 2006 (1) CTC 1 (K.Nithyanandam V. State o
Tamil Nadu), referring to the decision of the Apex Court reported i
AIR 2000 SC 2378 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies V
T.A.Kuttappan), this Court pointed out to the Co-operative Societies
functioning on democratic principle. Thus, given the object o
formation of a co-operative Society to work as a Society on democrati
principles born out of an agreement among the various members to for
the Society with a purpose to achieve the object of promoting th
economic welfare of the members, given the need for orderly growth an
development of this movement, the State is empowered to legislate
providing for incorporation, regulation and winding up of Co-operativ
Societies (Entry 32, List II of the Seventh Schedule to th
Constitution of India) in accordance with the Co-operative principles
such as, open membership democratic management, co-operation among co
operatives for the promotion of thrift, self-help, mutual aid amon
members with common socio-economic needs, to promote better busines
and better living. The Preamble to the Tamil Nadu Act thus emphasize
on the above aspect and as already seen, the Act provides on th
subject of registration of the Society with Bye laws, membership
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Constitution of the Board, power of the Board, General Body, windin
up, liability, remedial provision and other incidental and ancillar
matters, such as contents of the bye laws etc., while within th
framework of the byelaws. The Society has absolute authority to mak
byelaws on matters in consonance with the objects of the Society. I
also includes such provisions as are given under the Act, even thoug
the absence of referring to such provision in the bye-laws does no
make the bye-law bad in law, or that the bye laws alone have provide
for the particular topic, for, the bye laws are to be in consonanc
with the provisions of the Act and the Rules and any other la
applicable to the Society (Section 9(1)(e)), as an autonomous body t
regulate its affairs. It has every right to prescribe th
qualifications and eligibility of a Member to be admitted into
Society, apart from what is prescribed under the Act; that it is lef
to the authority of the Society to decide on the admission of a Membe
into a Society, even though such a Member may have satisfied th
eligibility criteria. In the context of such co-operative nature of
Society, Courts have held that formation of a Co-operative Society is
result of a consensus among persons; that it is out of a contractua
relationship that a Society is born and that the free will exercise
while forming the society continues even thereafter in conducting it
affairs and the business. In the background of the regulator
provisions of the Act, it is a settled law that the byelaws have n
statutory force; they cannot override the provisions of law, whic
means, the Society has to have the byelaws in terms of the provision
of the Act and the Rules made thereunder.
25. We have already seen that Section 21(2), as it originall
stood, provided for admission of members by the Board and after th
amendment, in the absence of the Board, the General Body is given th
authority to admit new members. We find that, in almost every one o
the byelaws of the Society produced, there is provision for admissio
of Members and the Board is given the authority to admit new Members
The question as to the admission of new members in terms of th
provisions of the Act admitting new members and the relevance of by
law, vis-a-vis, the provisions on the admission of members by th
State came up for consideration in the decisions reported in AIR 197
SC 966 (Damyanthi V. Union of India); (1984) 2 SCC 50 (Babaji Kondaj
Garad V. Nasik Merchants Co-operative Bank Ltd., Nasik and others
(2000) 9 SCC 295 (State of Maharashtra V. Karvenagar Sahakari Grih
Rachana Sanstha Maryadit) and (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co
operative Housing Society Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operativ
(Urban) and Others). On a question as to whether, through an amendmen
to the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societies Act, a Member could be thrus
on to the Society contrary to the byelaws, came up for consideration i
the decision reported in (1984) 2 SCC 50 (Babaji Kondaji Garad V. Nasi
Merchants Co-operative Bank Ltd., Nasik and others) (a decision cite
by the State). This decision also considered the question as t
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

whether the bye law has the force of a statute.
26. The brief facts of the case therein were that under Sectio
73-B of the Maharashtra Co-operative Societies Act, 1960, two seat
were to be reserved on the Committee of the Society for Member
belonging to Scheduled Caste or Scheduled Tribe and one for the weake
section of the Members, who have been granted loan from the Society, t
an amount not exceeding Rs.200/- during the year immediately preceding
The Section provided that the State Government may pass general o
special order directing such membership. The Section further provide
that if no persons are elected or appointed, the Committee shall co-op
the required number of members on the committee from amongst th
persons entitled to such representation.
27. On a challenge made to Section 73-B that it cannot hav
precedence over Bye law No.40, on a Special Leave petition filed befor
the Apex Court, the Supreme Court pointed out that Section 73 of th
Maharashtra Co-operative Societies Act provided for vesting of th
management of the Society in the Committee constituted in accordanc
with the Act, Rules and bye-laws. Section 73-B made it obligatory tha
two seats shall be reserved on the Committee of such Society or clas
of Societies, as the Government may, by general or special order
direct one for the members, who belong to Scheduled Caste or Schedule
Tribe and one for weaker sections of the Members. The Section itsel
pointed out the legislative intention that if no such persons ar
elected or appointed, the reserved seats may be filled in by co-option
This would imply that the General Body of Members will elect person
eligible to fill the reserved seats. Rejecting the contention of th
State that the method of filling of such reserved seats throug
election was not mandatory, it observed that such a view would "strik
a death-knell to the democratic principle of giving the constituenc
the right to elect its representatives and it would be usurped by
coterie of certain elected persons." The Apex Court further observe
"From enjoying a direct representation, the constituency would mov
backwards and the process of regress would be that instead of directio
election by the constituency which is the statutory right granted b
Section 73-B, the right to select would be usurped by the Board o
Director who would decide who should be co-opted to fill in th
reserved seats. Such a retrograde movement is undemocratic". Pointin
out that a Bye-law of a Co-operative Society could, at best, have th
status of an Articles of Association of a company governed by th
Companies Act, 1956 and that the Bye-law for Co-operative Societ
framed in pursuance of the provisions of the relevant Act cannot b
held to be law or to have the force of law, it observed that "they ar
neither statutory in character nor they have statutory flavour so as t
be raised as to the status of law. Now if there is any conflic
between a statute and the subordinate legislation, it does not requir
elaborate reasoning to firmly state that the statute prevails ove
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

subordinate legislation and the bye-law if not in conformity with th
statute in order to give effect to the statutory provisions, the rul
or bye-law has to be ignored. The statutory provision has precedenc
and must be complied with." Thus holding, the Supreme Court held tha
even though the bye-laws are not amended, if reserved seats remaine
unfilled by election, the same can be filled up by co-option. In th
circumstances, even though the Bye-law did not provide for reservatio
of seats, the prescription under Section 73B providing for reservatio
must receive such construction as would advance the purpose an
intendment underlying the provision, making reservation.
28. The Apex Court pointed out that in considering the effect o
the amendment, one must apply Heydon's test, namely, "(1) what was th
situation prior to the provision under construction, (2) what mischie
or defect was noticed before introducing the provision, (3) whether i
was remedial and (4) the reason for the remedy." Thus, while upholdin
the provision, the Apex Court pointed out that the Collector, whil
statutorily charged with a duty to hold election according to the Act
must specify in the election programme first, that there are reserve
seats to be filled up in the election and the class in whose favour th
reservation is made. Failure to do so would vitiate the election, i
being contrary to Section 73-B of the Maharashtra Co-operativ
Societies Act.
29. In the context of the admission of new members throug
statutory provision, the decision of the Constitution Bench of th
Supreme Court reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanthi V. Union of India
assumes great significance. The said decision was applied in th
decisions reported in (1997) 3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj V. M.L.Nagaraj) an
(2000) 6 SCC 127 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V
T.A.Kuttappan).
30. The brief facts of the decision reported in AIR 1971 SC 96
(Damyanthi V. Union of India) are that Hindi Sahitya Sammelan, a
association founded for development of Hindi and its propagatio
throughout the Country, was formed in the year 1910, registered as
Society under the Societies Registration Act. There were three classe
of Members, - Special Members, Permanent Members and Ordinary Members
As per the original Bye-laws, apart from constituting the Society
further members could be admitted under the above three classification
on being elected by the working committee of the Society. In the yea
1950, due to some differences among various members of the Society
there was an attempt to alter the constitution of the Society. Thi
led to litigation by filing of suits in Civil Court in Allahabad. Th
Uttar Pradesh Legislature passed an Act called UP Hindi Sahity
Sammelan Act No.36 of 1956, under which, a statutory body was create
under the name of Hindi Sahitya Sammelan. The new statutory Sammela
was to take over the management and the properties of the origina
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Hindi Sahitya Sammelan. The said Act was challenged before the Hig
Court, which held the State Act as violative of Article 19(1)(c) an
not saved by Article 19(4) of the Constitution of India
Thereafterwards, the Hindi Sahitya Sammelan Act No.13 of 1962 wa
passed in Entry 63, List I of the Seventh Schedule to the Constitutio
of India, which came up for challenge once again before the Apex Court
Under Section 4(1) of the Act, the Sammelan was to consist of the firs
members of the Sammelan and all persons, who, thereafter, were t
become members thereof, in accordance with the Rules made in tha
behalf. The first Governing Body was directed to make Rules in respec
of matters relating to membership including qualifications an
disqualifications for the membership of the Sammelan. The firs
Governing Body was to be constituted under Section 8 of the Act
consisting of a Chairman, Secretary and 13 other members. Th
Governing Body was constituted by a notification in the officia
capacity. The Rules made by the Governing Body relating to membershi
etc. were to be approved by the Central Government and were publishe
in such manner as the Government would direct. The Rules were to b
laid before the House of Parliament. The Rules came into force on 1
February, 1971. The Act was challenged on the ground that i
interfered with the right to form an association under Article 19(1)(c
and was not protected by Article 19(4) of the Constitution of India
The High Court held that since all the Members of the original Societ
had become Members of the newly formed Society under the Act, there wa
no infringement of right to form an association.
31. Apart from an appeal against the judgment of the High Court
Writ Petition against the Central Act was also filed by the origina
Society, apart from the Members of that Sammelan joining as partie
before the Supreme Court. On an examination of the provisions of th
Act, the Supreme Court pointed out that the Sammelan consisted not onl
of persons who were the original Members of the Society, but also o
others, who had been given the right to be Members of the Sammela
without the consent of the pre-existing Members, without any optio
available to the existing members of the Society to elect or refuse t
elect them as members, which was the right they possessed under th
Constitution of the Society itself. Referring to Section 12(1)(a
reserving the authority to the first Governing Body to make Rules i
respect of matters relating to Membership including qualification an
disqualification for membership of the Sammelan, the Apex Court pointe
out that the Sammelan, which came into existence under the impugne
legislation, was not identical with the Sammelan, which was registere
originally under the Societies Registration Act, 1860. The admissio
of the future members was no longer at the choice of the origina
members, who had formed an association. The original members had n
right to induct new members. Thus the Apex Court held that the Ac
interfered with the right to form an association, which was exercise
by the Members of the Society by forming the Society with it
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

constitution; the future members could come in as members only as
result of the election by the Working Committee; there was no identit
between the original society and the society formed under the impugne
Act. Dealing with Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution of India, th
Apex Court held "it is true that it has been held by this Court tha
after an Association has been formed and the right under under Articl
19(1)(c) has been exercised by the members forming it, they have n
right to claim that its activities must also be permitted to be carrie
on in the manner they desire."
32. Distinguishing all these cases cited by the State, the Ape
Court pointed out that with the old Society still existing and so to
the Governing Body, the impugned Act, apart from regulating th
administration of the affairs of the Society, altered the compositio
of the society itself by admitting members, who had been imposed a
members by the Act and in whose admission to membership, the member
who formed a society, had no say. This amounted to alteration in th
composition of the Society itself; hence, was an interference with th
right to continue to function as members of the association, which wa
voluntarily formed by the original founders. It observed that th
right to form an association necessarily implies that the person
forming the association have also the right to continue to b
associated with only those whom they voluntarily admit in th
association. "Any law, by which members are introduced in the voluntar
association without any option being given to the members to keep the
out or any liability, which takes away the membership of those who hav
voluntarily joined it, will be a law violating the right to form a
association." It rejected the arguments of the State that the righ
guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c) is confined to the initial stage o
forming an association; hence does not protect the right to continu
the association with the membership either chosen by the founders o
regulated by the rules made by the association itself. "The right ca
be effective only if it is held to include within it the right t
continue the association with its composition as voluntarily agree
upon by the persons forming the association". The Apex Court pointe
out that the impugned Act purported to take away all the properties o
the Society, leaving the Society as an existing body, and given them t
the new sammelan, which was a separate and distinct legal entity fro
the Society. Thus the law depriving the Society of its propertie
altogether, could not be held to be a reasonable restriction, in th
public interest. Referring to the power given to the Governing Body
the Supreme Court pointed out that the admission of such members unde
the Act could even leave the original members in a small minority. I
the context of the Rules thus made, divesting the properties from th
original Sammelan to a new entity and the identity of the new Sammela
not identical with the Sammelan originally registered under th
Societies Registration Act and that no discretion was left to th
Society in the matter of admission of members, the Apex Court observe
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

that the Act was in violation of Article 19(1)(c), which seriousl
interfered with the right of citizens to form a Society. Thus the Act
which materially altered the original association, was declared a
unconstitutional and struck down by the Apex Court.
33. The judgment of the Supreme Court shows that it lays down th
proposition that a Co-operative Society is a voluntary body, into whic
there should be no admission of new members without any option bein
given to the members to keep them out; the right to form an associatio
includes the right to continue to be associated with members whom the
voluntarily admit in the association. The said decision is a
authority for the proposition that if members are thrust on th
Society, the spirit of co-operative movement would be put an end to an
it would be in violation of the Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution o
India.
34. In the decision reported in (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co
operative Housing Society Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operativ
(Urban) and Others), the Supreme Court once again dealt with th
similar issue. The said case relates to a Society formed with th
object of providing housing to the members of the Parsi community. Th
Society was registered in terms of Section 11 of the Bombay Co
operative Societies Act. The Bye-law provided the objects of th
Society to carry on trade of building and of buying, selling hiring
letting and developing land in accordance with co-operative principles
The Bye-law provided that members belonging to Parsi community shall b
elected by the Committee of the Society, subject to satisfying othe
conditions in the Bye-law. The transfer of share allotted to a membe
had to have the previous sanction of the Committee of the Society. I
is seen that one of the members of the Society sold the plot, in whic
he had constructed a residential building, to another member with th
previous sanction of the Society. The said Member sought fo
permission of the Society to demolish the bungalow and put up
commercial building in its place. The same was refused by the Societ
by quoting the Bye-law, which did not permit commercial use of th
land. Hence, the said member sought for permission to construc
residential flats to be sold to Parsis. It is seen that though th
permission was granted to construct residential flats to be sold onl
to the members of the Parsi community, the said Member did not act o
the permission for a period of seven years. However, the said Membe
was stated to have started negotiation with Builders' Association, i
violation of the restriction on the sale of shares or property to
non-Parsi. This gave rise to a litigation and an order of injunctio
was sought for against the Society. The Board informed that th
Society could not restrict its membership only to the Parsi communit
and that the membership should remain open for every person. Based o
that, once again the Member applied for permission, which was rejecte
by the Society on the ground that it was contrary to the Rules, Act an
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

the byelaws of the Society.
35. On a Writ Petition filed as against the Tribunal's order
that the view of the Society was a restriction on the right t
property, the same was dismissed by the High Court. Hence, an appea
was preferred before the Apex Court. After referring to the decisio
reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanthi V. Union of India), which wa
distinguished by the decision reported in AIR 1985 SC 973 (Daman Sing
V. State of Punjab), the Supreme Court pointed out that the Rule woul
prevail over the Bye laws of the Society, which confined the membershi
to only persons belonging to the Parsi Community. On going through th
provisions contained in Section 24(1) of the Bombay Act, the Suprem
Court pointed out that it did not contemplate an open membership, d
hors the byelaws of the Society, nor it had precluded the Society fro
prescribing the qualification for membership based on a belief
persuasion or religion for that matter. The individual right of
member got submerged in the collective right of the Society. Thus th
Supreme Court referred to the decision reported in (1997) 3 SCC 68
(U.P.V.C.O.D. Chheoki Employees' Coop. Society Ltd.), holding thus:
"His being a member of the society is subject to the
operation of the Act, rules and bye-laws applicable from
time to time. A member of the society has no independent
right qua the society and it is the society that is
entitled to represent as the corporate aggregate. No
individual member is entitled to assail the
constitutionality of the provisions of the Act, rules and
the bye-laws as he has his right under the Act, rules and
the bye-laws and is subject to its operation. The stream
cannot rise higher than the source."
36. The Apex Court held that in the absence of any prohibition i
the Act against the formation of a Society for a particular communit
or origin, it could not be held that the confining of membership, a
was done by the Bye-law, was opposed to public policy. It furthe
observed "Membership in a co-operative society only brings about
contractual relationship among the members forming it subject of cours
to the Act and the Rules. One becomes a member in a co-operativ
society either at the time of its formation or acquires membership i
it on possessing the requisite qualification under the bye-laws of th
society and on being accepted as a member. It is not as if one has
fundamental right to become a member of a co-operative society. Bu
certainly, if the application of one for membership, who is otherwis
qualified to be a member under the Act, Rules and the bye-laws of th
society, is rejected unreasonably or for frivolous reasons, the perso
may be entitled to enforce his claim to become a member in a
appropriate forum or court of law. This is the effect of the decisio
in Jain Merchants Co-operative Housing Society vs. HUF of Manubha
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

(1995 (1) Gujarat Law Reporter 19), relied on by the High Court. Th
said decision does not lay down a proposition, nor can it lay down
proposition, that even a person who does not qualify to be a member i
terms of the bye-laws of a society can enforce a right to become
member of that society. It is one thing to say that it is not desirabl
to restrict membership in a society based solely on religion or sex bu
it is quite different thing to say that any such voluntary approve
bye-law containing such a restriction could be ignored or declare
unconstitutional by an authority or a Tribunal created under the Ac
itself. Normally, the bye-laws of a society do not have the status of
statute and as held by this Court in Coop. Central Credit Bank Ltd. Vs
Industrial Tribunal, Hyderabad (AIR 1970 SC 245), bye-laws are only th
rules which govern the internal management or administration of
society and they are of the nature of articles of association of
company incorporated under the Companies Act. They may be bindin
between the persons affected by them but they do not have the force o
a statute."
37. The Apex Court further observed that while the validity of
Bye-law has to be tested in the light of the provisions of the Act an
the Rules governing the Co-operative Societies, it is not permissibl
for the State Government to compel the Society to amend its Bye laws
as it would defeat the object of formation of a Society. Referring t
the decision reported in AIR 1989 Bombay 392 (Karvenagar Sahakari Grih
Rachana Sanstha Maryadit V. State of Maharashtra), which wa
subsequently confirmed in the decision reported in (2000) 9 SCC 29
(State of Maharashtra V. Karvenagar Sahakari Griha Rachana Sansth
Maryadit), the Apex Court observed that the paramount consideration o
the State lies in the interests of the Society. What is in th
interest of the Society is primarily for the Society alone to decid
and it is not for the outside agency to have a say on this. Where th
Registrar exercised his statutory power to amend the byelaws, suc
directions should satisfy the requirement of the interests of th
Society. It observed that the approved byelaws clearly confer power o
the Committee to reject the application for membership of a person wh
is not qualified in terms of the Bye-law and the same cannot b
interfered with on the basis of anything contained in the Act or th
Rules and in the name of public policy, a registered Society cannot b
directed to admit a member, who is not otherwise qualified to be
member in terms of the duly registered byelaws. As to the nature o
the byelaws of a Society, the Supreme Court pointed out "the byelaws o
a Co-operative Society setting out the terms of membership to it, is
contract entered into by a person when he seeks to become a member o
that society. Even the formation of the society is based on a contract
This freedom to contract available to a citizen cannot be curtailed o
curbed relying on the fundamental rights enshrined in Part III of th
Constitution of India against State action."
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

38. Touching on the rights of a member admitted to a Society, th
Supreme court pointed out that when a person exercised his right o
association to become a member of a Society by entering into a contrac
with others, he submerges his rights in the common right to be enjoye
by all. Thus, he is really exercising his right of associatio
guaranteed by Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution of India. In tha
process, his rights merge in the rights of the society and ar
controlled by the Act and the byelaws of the Society. The availabilit
of membership is subject to the qualification prescribed under th
provisions of the Act, Rules and the byelaws and if the relevant Bye
law places any restriction on a person admitted to a co-operativ
society, that Bye-law would be operative against him. Thus the Ape
Court upheld the right of the Society that the property was to be deal
with by a member, only in terms of the byelaws of the Society.
39. The above-said judgments thus laid down that the byelaw
framed by the Society are in the nature of contract entered int
between persons joining together to form a society; the various clause
of the bye laws must be in consonance with the provisions of the Ac
and that they are in addition to what is prescribed under the Act an
the Rules. As forming of a Society itself is the result of th
voluntary act of individuals, admission of new members is also a matte
within the choice and option of the members and whenever such option i
exercised under the Act, the same is violative of Article 19(1)(c) o
the Constitution of India. Apart from the Statute laying down th
qualifications and disqualifications, the bye law prescribes th
qualification of the Members to be admitted therein. No provision o
the Act can compel admission of a member, contrary to the bye laws o
the Society. The above decisions are also an authority for th
proposition that the State cannot impose membership into the Societ
and against the free will of the society which would bring in a chang
in the composition of the Society, since it fundamentally tinkers wit
the independence of the society and the democratic control that th
society exercises in the matter of selecting the members with whom th
society desires to associate. Such compulsion would go against th
democratic spirit with which the society functions. The interests o
the individual member on admission submerges with that of the society
The judgments are also an authority for the proposition that th
byelaws are not statutory in character and they cannot run against th
provisions of the Act and the Rules.
40. In the background of the law laid down by the Apex Court, th
provisions of the Tamil Nadu Act has to be seen. We have seen in th
preceding paragraphs that Rule 6 of the Rules lays down the contents o
the subject matter of a Bye-law of a Society. The eligibility criteri
of a person to become a Member for admission into a Society is governe
by the Bye–laws, Act and the Rules. Section 21(2)(i) conditions th
admission of a person as a member, subject to his satisfying th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

criteria laid down in the Bye-laws, Act and the Rules. Thus, when th
Society makes Bye-laws on subjects enumerated under Rule 6, this is i
addition to what is already provided for under the Act and within th
framework of the provisions of the Act and the Rules; what is to b
provided for as laid down under the Act and the Rules are not contrar
to what is provided in the Act. Thus, while the right to form a
association is guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution o
India to the citizens, Article 43-B and Article 243ZI enjoin on th
State to make provisions with respect to incorporation, regulation an
winding up of the Co-operative Societies based on the principles o
voluntary formation, democratic member control, member economi
participation and autonomous functioning and thereby, promote voluntar
functioning, democratic control and professional management of the Co
operative Societies.
41. In the decision reported in 1978 (1) Karnataka Law Journal 30
(H. Puttappa and Ors. Vs. The State of Karnataka and Ors.), the Ful
Bench of the Karnataka High Court pointed out that when the principle
are not affected, there is no question of the fundamental righ
guaranteed by Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution of India bein
impaired or infringed. The Full Bench of the Karnataka High Cour
considered this question in the context of Section 14-A of th
Karnataka Co-operative Societies Act as to whether it conferre
unguided arbitrary power on the Registrar directing amalgamation of Co
operative Societies of more than 800 societies. Holding that th
powers conferred on the authority were not violative of Article 14 o
the Constitution of India, the Karnataka High Court pointed out to th
locus standi of the new members challenging the provisions relatin
to amalgamation. Referring to the Section 14-A, the Karnataka Hig
Court held that the amalgamation, division or reorganisation of co
operative societies could be ordered only when the statutor
authorities are satisfied that such action is essential either i
public interest or in the interest of co-operative movement or for th
purpose of securing the proper management of any co-operative society
The Section, therefore, not only lays down the policy, but als
provides the safeguards to ensure that the cooperative societies ar
not dismantled unnecessarily.
42. On the issue raised that the said provision encroaches th
right under Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution of India, that withou
their consent, they had been made members of the amalgamated Co
operative Society, the Karnataka High Court pointed out that as soon a
the citizens form an association, the Members cannot lay claim to th
fundamental right guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c); the associatio
cannot complain about the restrictions in the Act as restriction
against the right conferred by Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution o
India. It pointed out "The provisions in the Act govern the duties an
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

privileges, rights and liabilities, property and funds, the audit
inspection and surcharge, the dissolution and amalgamation of co
operative societies and its members. The Act thus regulates th
economic interests of the members and controls the trading activitie
of the societies in the interests of the public or in the interests o
the advancement of the co-operative movement. The process o
amalgamation and division or reorganisation of co-operative societie
relate only to structural alterations in the corporate bodies an
cannot be construed as a restriction on the right guaranteed to th
citizens by Article 19(1)(c)." The High Court also referred to th
decision reported in (1971) 3 SCR 840 (Damayanti V. Union of India) an
held that the facts of that case are far removed from the case on han
and held that the Act merely regulates the economic interests of th
Members and hence, the process of amalgamation relating to structura
alteration in the corporate body cannot be construed as a restrictio
on the right guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution o
India. As far as this decision is concerned, even though the view o
the High Court that the right to form a Society is only a statutor
right would no longer hold waters, in view of Article 19(1)(c) as i
stands today, yet, the view of the High Court on the regulatory natur
of the Act that on being members, they cease to have any individua
interest other than that of the Societies' interest, merits acceptanc
in the context of the protection available in Section 14-A of th
Andhra Pradesh Co-operative Societies Act and the regulation brough
