was in the economic interests of the members as well as in publi
interest. Therefore, regulations which promotes the interests of th
society are valid. So long as the action taken note of the voluntar
nature of functioning of the association and in the given case, th
same is regulated by the statute in public interest, which is a subjec
matter of subjective satisfaction in an objective way, the authority o
the State to regulate the affairs cannot be faulted with, as violativ
of the constitutional mandate. In such cases, the question of violenc
to the provisions of the bye laws cannot arise, but must yield to th
larger public interest that the society seeks to promote.
43. In the context of the byelaws prescribing qualification fo
admission of members and the authority given to the Board to admit ne
members, the principle of democratic control exercised by the c
operative societies, the decisions of the Apex Court reported in (1997
3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj and another V. M.L.Nagaraj and others) an
(2000) 6 SCC 127 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V
T.A.Kuttappan) need to be seen.
44. Both these decisions relate to the question as to whether th
Administrator, who was a stop-gap arrangement to manage the day-to-da
administration, could take policy decision on admitting new members
The Supreme Court pointed out that when the State appointed a
Administrator superseding the Committee, the power of the Administrato
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

should be confined within the parameters set out under the provision
of the Act, Rules and the byelaws and that the Administrator has n
power to enrol new members. He can only organise election process i
accordance with the provisions of the Act, Rules and the byelaws of th
Society. The decision of the Full Bench of this Court reported in 200
(1 ) CTC 1 (K.Nithyanandam V. State of Tamil Nadu) followed the above
said decisions to hold that the Special Officer has no authority t
admit new members and being an in-charge officer, his authority is t
only go ahead with the day-to-day functioning of the Society.
45. Thus the decisions, right from the year 1971, i.e., AIR 197
SC 966 (Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India) to (2005) 5 SCC 63
(Zoroastrian Co-operative Housing Society Ltd. V. District Registrar
Co-operative (Urban) and Others), as well as the cases relating to th
Special Officer admitting members, are concerned with situation
brought out by the admission of members who are ineligible to b
admitted as members as well as cases on ignoring the options availabl
to the existing members to decide on the admissibility of the members
that there was no scope for the exercise of free will to decide wit
whom they should be associated with. In the decision reported in (2011
9 SCC 286 (A.P.Dairy Development Corpn. Federation Vs. B.Narasimh
Reddy), after considering the decisions reported in AIR 1971 SC 96
(Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India) and (2004) 1 SCC 712 (Dharam Dut
& Ors vs Union Of India & Ors), the Apex Court observed "it become
evident that the right of the citizens to form the association ar
different from running the business by that association. Therefore
right of individuals to form a society has to be understood in
completely different context. Once a co-operative society is formed an
registered, for the reason that co-operative society itself is
creature of the statute, the rights of the society and that of it
members stand abridged by the provisions of the Act. The activities o
the society are controlled by the statute. Therefore, there cannot b
any objection to statutory interference with their composition o
functioning merely on the ground of contravention of individual's righ
of freedom of association by statutory functionaries." Thus the cas
before us is of a totally different nature.
46. A reading of the byelaws placed before this Court shows tha
apart from laying down the objects of the Society, the share capital o
the Society, the eligibility criteria of the Society and the busines
of the Society, it also contains clauses on the eligibility of a perso
seeking admission, ineligibility of a person from being admitted an
the expulsion, which are verbatim taken from the Act itself. Thus th
Bye-laws provide for the authority of the Board to admit new members i
terms of the provisions contained in Section 21(2), as it stoo
originally. The provisions contained in Section 21(2) on the admissio
of new members is a regulatory provision, well within the framework o
the concept adumbrated in the decision reported in AIR 1971 SC 96
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

(Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India), preserving the rights of th
existing members to admit or reject an application for membership
Thus, even with the ultimate authority of the registered Societ
resting on the General Body, when the Act conferred the authority o
the admission of a new member on the Governing Body, if any Bye-law i
to provide for the authority on the General Body to admit new members
even going by the principle of voluntariness, the Bye-law bein
contrary to the provisions of the Act, would be bad in the eye of law
Thus, regulating the exercise of the authority as to the admission o
members cannot be viewed as a matter exclusively within the authorit
of the Society and the State has no authority to regulate a situatio
warranted by the absence of the Governing Body. If the State can mak
provisions for empowering a Body to admit new members, it is equall
open to the State to regulate the situation warranted by the absence o
the Body on whom the authority stands; that till such time the origina
authority is constituted, a stop-gap arrangement has to be made throug
another body. The contention of the petitioners herein is that th
State cannot make such an amendment, which would be against the teno
of Part IX-B of the Constitution brought about under the 97
th amendmen
to the Constitution.
47. A reading of the amendment brought in to Article 19(1)(c)
Article 43-B and Part IXB of the Constitution of India, with effec
from 15.2.2012, shows the anxiety of the State to promote voluntar
formation, autonomous functioning, democratic control and provisiona
amendment of Co-operative Societies. Article 43-B in Chapter IV
Directive Principles of State policy was inserted by the Constitutio
(97
th Amendment) Act, 2011. In furtherance of this, is Part IX-B -Th
Co-operative Societies. It contains Articles 243-ZH to 243-ZT. 'Board
is defined under Article 243ZH(b) to mean the board of directors or th
governing body of a co-operative society, by whatever name called, t
which the direction and control of the management of the affairs of
society is entrusted to. Sub-section (c) defines 'co-operativ
society', which means, a society registered or deemed to be registere
under any law relating to co-operative societies for the time being i
force in any State. Article 243ZI speaks about the State makin
provisions with respect to the incorporation, regulation and winding u
of co-operative societies based on the principles of voluntar
formation, democratic member-control, member-economic participation an
autonomous functioning. Part IXB also lays down the number and term o
the members of the Board and its office bearers. Although the numbe
of Directors may be as prescribed by the Legislature of the State, yet
Article 243ZJ fixes the maximum number as not exceeding 21. It speak
about the reservation of one seat for SC/ST and two seats for women o
the Board of every co-operative society. The term of office of electe
members of the board is given as five years from the date of election
The proviso to sub-Article (2) to Article 243ZJ states "Provided tha
the board may fill a casual vacancy on the board by nomination out o
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

the same class of members in respect of which the casual vacancy ha
arisen, if the term of office of the board is less than half of it
original term."
48. The Section also provides for co-option of persons to be th
members of the board having experience in the field of banking
management, finance or specialisation in any other field relating t
the objects and activities undertaken by the co-operative society, a
members of the board of such society. However the number of such co
opted members shall not exceed two in addition to twenty-one Directors
It also specifies the right of the co-opted members not to vote in th
election of the co-operative society. Article 243ZK of th
Constitution of India deals with the election of members of the board
Article 243ZL of the Constitution of India deals with Supersession an
suspension of the Board and interim management with a limitation of
period not exceeding six months for the suspension or supersession t
operate. Article 243ZM deals with auditing of accounts of co-operativ
societies. Article 243ZN provides for convening of general bod
meetings of the Society within a period of six months of the closure o
the financial year to transact the business as may be provided in suc
law. Article 243ZQ deals with Offences and penalties to be provide
for by the Legislature of a State. Article 243ZT provides for th
continuance of the existing laws, which are not inconsistent with th
provisions of Part I until the expiration of one year from th
commencement or until amended or repealed by the competent Legislature
whichever is less.
49. Thus a reading of the Articles inserted in Chapter IXB of th
Constitution of India shows the necessity for constituting
representative Board, having representatives of the weaker Sections o
the Society as well as professionals to give the society the advantage
of professional management, the need for an autonomous functioning o
the Society by holding election before the term expired, restrictin
the period of suspension to six months and the need to hold an electio
and the need for placing the business of the society before the Genera
Body Meeting within a period of six months of the close of th
financial year.
50. While introducing the amending Act, the Minister fo
Agriculture referred to the amendment brought to Article 19(1)(c) o
the Constitution of India to include the right to form a Co-operativ
Society and the consequential amendments and pointed out that suc
insertion would give boost to the co-operative movement. Referring t
the prevailing status on the Societies' functioning without election
for years, which seriously affect the democratic member control an
autonomous functioning, the Minister stated "the proposed amendmen
would go a long way to address the problems and deficiencies that th
cooperatives face today"; that the co-operative movement would b
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

further strengthened by the amendment and serve the members
particularly those from economically weaker sections on board. Th
amendment to the Constitution thus touches on five important aspects
namely, on voluntary formation, autonomous functioning, democrati
member-control, member-economic participation.
51. In the background of the amendment thus brought into th
Constitution, the various contentions raised by the petitioners as t
the constitutionality of the amendment to Section 21(2) need to b
seen.
52. The contentions of the petitioners fall under three heads
namely, (i) constitutionality of the amendment made to Section 21(2
that it is offensive of Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution of India
the said provision is unreasonable, arbitrary and not a reasonabl
restriction to be saved under Article 19(4) of the Constitution o
India. It is violative of Part IX B of the Constitution. Considerin
the objects and reasons on the 97
th amendment to the Constitution, whic
prescribes the Authority of the Governing Body, the amendmen
interferes with the democratic set up, democratic control an
professional management of the Co-operative Societies; hence agains
the spirit of the Constitution (97
th amendment) Act, 2011. The admissio
of new members is the prerogative of the Governing Body, which i
empowered under the byelaw to do so. Hence, the amendment interfere
with the byelaws of the Society and hence against the spirit o
cooperative movement. The amendment, thus being contrary to th
decisions of law declared by the Apex Court reported in AIR 1971 SC 96
(Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India) followed in (2000) 6 SCC 12
(Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V. T.A.Kuttappan
and (1997) 3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj V. M.L.Nagaraj) and in the decisio
reported in (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co-operative Housing Societ
Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operative (Urban) and Others), is a
attempt to nullify the decision of this Court reported in 2006 (1) CT
1 (K.Nithyanandam Vs. State of Tamil Nadu). The admission of ne
members by the General Body would affect the composition of th
association. The Board is a specialised body, which alone is competen
to assess the merits of the eligible applicants to become members o
the Society. When the byelaws say that the new members are to b
admitted by the Board alone, it is not open to the State to confer suc
authority on the General Body. Going by the second proviso to Sectio
21(2) of the Act providing for deemed admission, there is ever
possibility of unmerited members entering into the Society, thereb
affecting the composition of the Society. The amendment, in effect
seeks to bypass the judgment of this Court reported in 2006 (1) CTC
(K.Nithyanandam V. State of Tamil Nadu) and what could not be achieve
through Section 89A of the Act, the State seeks to achieve the same b
the amendment and hence, the amended act is a colourable piece o
legislation and is contrary to the decision reported in 2006 (1) CTC
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

(K.Nithyanandam V. State of Tamil Nadu). The above are the commo
contentions of the petitioners.
53. Mr.P.Wilson, learned senior counsel appearing for th
petitioners in W.P.Nos.25699, 25882, 25889 and 25890 of 2012, submitte
that when the Bye-law of the Society contemplated admission of ne
members by the Board alone, the amendment giving authority to th
general body is aimed at making inroads into the functioning of the Co
operative Society, thereby affecting the autonomy of the Co-operativ
Society. When this Court has specifically held in the decision reporte
in 2006 (1) CTC 1 (K.Nithyanandam V. State of Tamil Nadu) that th
Board alone has the authority to admit new members and that the Specia
Officer has no right to admit a member, the present amendment unde
Section 21(2) of the Act is an attempt to get over the judgment of thi
Court, thereby, nullify the same. Thus, what the State could no
achieve through Section 89A of the Act, is now sought to be achieved b
the State through the amendment to have a control over the admission o
members, thereby, undermining the democratic principles on which th
Society is formed.
54. Commenting that the State has no right to bring in new member
to the Society, admission of members into the Society being
prerogative right of the Board, the amendment to Section 21(2) of th
Act is a clear violation of Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution o
India. Even going by the explanatory statement, it is clear that th
intention of bringing an amendment is only to bring in the members o
its choice into the Society, thereby restricting the authority of th
Society to select its own members. This would totally change th
composition of the Society and have serious impact on the functionin
of the Society; hence, the amendment is violative of the spirit of th
97
th amendment to the Constitution. When the aim of the 97th amendmen
is to promote voluntary formation by free exercise of will for a
automonous formation for self-governance and to have democrati
control through the elected members of the Board as well as to brin
about a professional management through qualified persons, the Stat
cannot use its authority to bring an amendment, which would not be i
furtherance of Article 43-B of the Constitution of India. On the othe
hand, it would be totally violative of Article 19(1)(c) of th
Constitution. He further pointed out that the Board is a qualifie
body, which alone has the expertise and knowledge to select th
eligible members, whose presence would contribute and promote the well
being of the Society. Thus, when the Board is not in existence, givin
authority to the General Body would only result in bringing in members
which may be adverse to the well-being of the Society. Hence, th
very complexity of the Society would be interfered with. He furthe
pointed out that the Board is a specialised body to assess the merit
of an eligible member to be admitted into the society and a Genera
Body is not well-informed in that regard to judge the merits of a
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

individual applicant to be admitted into the Society. When the mandat
of the Constitution is to bring the election within a period of si
months, instead of holding the election immediately, the amendmen
brought in is an attempt to interfere and stifle the democratic contro
of the Society.
55. Referring to Section 33 of the Co-operative Societies Act
which touches on the composition of the Board and the meetings of th
Board, he submitted that in the absence of any amendment to Section 3
of the Act, the State cannot bring in General Body as a body competen
to admit new members. Referring to the hurried manner with which th
Special Officers have called for the General Body Meeting to admit ne
members, he pointed out to the Constitutional amendment made o
15.2.2012, particularly Part IXB, Article 43-B and Article 19(1)(c) o
the Constitution of India. The State amended Section 21(2) of the Act
effective from 16.11.2012. Form No.16 is the relevant form relating t
admission of new members. Rule 27 is the relevant Rule prescribing th
application to be submitted by a person intending to become a member
the manner of admission as a Member. The said Rule and Form 16 wer
amended in tune with the amendment to Section 21(2) only on 19.11.2012
However, the Meetings on the admission of new members were held eve
before the amendment to the Rule and the form were brought in an
hence, the election based on the unamended Form 16 are not valid. Thus
the admission of new members could only be on the basis of a prope
application in Form 16; hence, the admission of new members on th
notice sent by the Special Officer holding the General Body Meeting, i
ab initio void. He further submitted that the State cannot control th
Society to amend its byelaws to empower the General Body to admit ne
members.
56. In this connection, he placed reliance on the decision
reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India
(2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co-operative Housing Society Ltd. V
District Registrar, Co-operative (Urban) and Others), (2000) 6 SCC 12
(Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V. T.A.Kuttappan
and (2009) 5 SCC 342 (Grand Kakatiya Sheraton Hotel and Tower
Employees and Workers Union v. Srinivasa Resorts Limited and Ors.) an
submitted that the provisions are arbitrary, unreasonable, unworkabl
and contrary to the law declared by the Apex Court and violative o
Articles 14 and 19(1)(c) of the amendment brought under 97
th amendmen
to the Constitution in Chapter I and the byelaws made by the Societ
and hence, liable to be struck down.
57. Learned senior counsel further pointed out that the State ha
not understood the spirit of the 97
th amendment, which is clearl
brought out in the speech of the Minister. He pointed out that Par
IXA clearly brings out the demarcation of the power between the Boar
and the General Body; that the Board is the ultimate authority in th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

matter of managing the affairs of the Society; the voluntary formation
independent functioning, democratic control and professional managemen
cannot be compromised at all by induction of new members through th
General Body. Pointing out that there is absolutely no necessity at al
to amend the provisions hurriedly through an ordinance, he submitte
that the only mandate which the State ought to have taken up emergentl
as per the 97
th amendment, is the conduct of the election. Whe
admission of new member is essentially in the realm of the Society an
that the method by which such members could be admitted is laid down b
the byelaws, the amendment is clearly an interference with th
democratic set up of the Society. Thus, he submitted that the visio
of 97
th amendment must be sustained at any cost and the State canno
dilute the directive principles as given under Article 43-B of th
Constitution of India. In this connection, he placed reliance on th
decision reported in (2009) 5 SCC 342 (Grand Kakatiya Sheraton Hote
and Towers Employees and Workers Union v. Srinivasa Resorts Limited an
Ors.), particularly paragraph Nos.73 and 77 and submitted that th
byelaws cannot be interfered with by the amendment. Thus, the byela
cannot be subjected to any control or amended through the Act. Whe
the right to form an association is a constitutional right, the righ
to continue the association as per the byelaws is also a constitutiona
right. In this connection, he referred to the decisions reported i
(2000) 6 SCC 127 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V
T.A.Kuttappan) and AIR 1971 SCC 966 (Damyanti Naranga v. Union o
India), 2006 (1) CTC 1 (K.Nithyanandam V. State of Tamil Nadu
particularly Paragraph No.29 and (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co
operative Housing Society Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operativ
(Urban) and Others).
58. Referring to the decision reported in AIR 1989 Bombay 39
(Karvenagar Sahakari Griha Rachana Sanstha Maryadir and Anr. V. Stat
of Maharashtra and ors.) particularly, paragraph 9, he pointed out tha
if the members are to be admitted through the General Body, this call

https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

V. State of Tamil Nadu), particularly Paragraph No.49 relating to th

72. Countering the submissions made by the petitioners, learne
Advocate General appearing for the State pointed out that for the pas
11 years, the day-to-day affairs of all the societies are managed onl
by the Special Officer and there was no election conducted; hence ther
are no boards constituted. With the result, no new members ar
admitted into any cooperative society, thereby paralysing the ver
movement. The general body is a perpetual body in existence. Thus, i
the concept of cooperative formation and democratic functioning of
society to carry on the movement further has to have any meanin
necessarily, there must be some instrumentality which must exercis
authority to admit members to join the society. Thus, he referred t
Article 243ZI of the Constitution of India as to the need for makin
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

laws to provide for the incorporation, regulation and winding up of th
cooperative societies based on the principles of democratic membe
control of the society. The present amendment seeks to achieve thi
constitutional mandate. Further, the supremacy of the general bod
over that of the Board - the governing body, cannot, in any manner, b
undermined at any point of time on the singular fact that the bye la
states that the board shall have the authority to admit new member
which is nothing but the mandate in the Section. He further submitte
that byelaw is only a Regulation of the society for the interna
administration of the society. Being registered under the provisions o
the Act, every byelaw has to be in consonance with the provisions o
the Act and within the framework of the Act and the Rules. Th
decisions of the Courts clearly point out that byelaws are not in th
nature of statutory Rules. Even though the byelaws may provide for th
eligibility criteria, even that has to be in tune with the provision
of the Act. The procedure and the authority for admission of a ne
member satisfying the eligibility requirement is given under the Ac
and the Rules. Thus, the amended provisions in Section 21(2) provide
for admission of new members through the Board and in the absence of
governing body, this jurisdiction is exercised by the general body. H
further submitted that being a State subject falling under Entry 3
List II of 7
th Schedule, the Constitution has left it to the State t
legislate on the subject of cooperative societies, making provision
with respect to incorporation, regulation and winding-up of th
cooperative societies based on the voluntary formation, democrati
member control, member economic participation and autonomou
functioning. Article 43B enjoins on the State to actively promot
voluntary formation, autonomous functioning, democratic control an
professional management of the cooperative societies. Thus, keeping i
tune with the democratic formation, autonomous functioning an
democratic control, the State has passed this amendment, which is wel
within the Constitution manadte. Considering the 97th amendmen
contemplating holding of election in furtherance of these democrati
principles, admission of new members cannot be frowned upon by th
petitioners as interfering with the byelaws of the society or contrar
to the democratic principles. The amendment made to Section 21(2) i
only in furtherance of the spirit of 97th amendment to the constitutio
of India, to re-activate and revitalise the cooperative movement. Th
provision thus contemplates the general body admitting new members onl
when there is no governing body functioning. Wherever the governin
body is there, it is not open to the general body to assum
jurisdiction. Hence, the provision made under Section 21(2
permitting the general body to admit new members in the absence of
governing body, is transitory in nature. The amendment provides for
lacuna in the Act and in the byelaws and hence, the regulator
amendment made is well within the legislative authority of the State
He further pointed out to the Full Bench decision of this Court
holding that the Special Officer does not have the authority to admi
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

members and that the general body is the ultimate authority in
society. Thus, when there is no other authority available competent t
admit new members, with the stalemate on admission of new members
amendment to Section 21(2) is necessary in promoting the democrati
functioning of the society. Relying on paragraph 49 of the decision o
this Court reported in 2006 (1) CTC 1 (K.Nithiyanantham Vs. State o
Tamil Nadu and others), he submitted that the Full Bench decision ha
not, in fact, pointed out that the general body alone is empowered t
admit new member. As such, contrary to the assertion of th
petitioners, the Full Bench of this Court, in fact, had pointed out t
the supremacy of the authority of the general body to admit ne
members. Commenting on the stand of the petitioners that the Boar
consists of specialised persons, who alone are competent to admit ne
members, he pointed out that the Board consists of members elected onl
from the general body; therefore, the board members are the member
in the general body; in the circumstances, the question of attributin
any better wisdom or an expertise or qualifications to the boar
governing body alone over the general body, does not arise; The Boar
is only the reflection of the general body; consequently, there is n
illegality or arbitrariness or violation in the constitutiona
provisions in enacting an amendment to Section 21(2) of the Tamil Nad
Cooperative Societies Act. He pointed out that in providing for such
mechanism, there is no undermining of the democratic control by th
members, who alone are to exercise their free will, admitting ne
members. Consequently, the question of violation Article 19(1)(c) o
the Constitution of India does not arise at all. He further pointe
out that the purpose of the amendment has to be seen in the context o
the background of the legislation and the defect that the amendmen
sought to remedy.
73. Referring to the powers of the General Body, as evident fro
the various provisions of the Act, he pointed out that the ultimat
authority in a society is the General Body; hence, even if there be n
amendment, if there is no governing body existing, the general body i
empowered to admit the new members and the amendment merely carried ou
the law laid down by the Full Bench and that the amendment is not a
exercise to go against the law laid down by the Full Bench of thi
Court or to get over the law declared by the Full Bench. Referring t
the reliance placed on the decision reported in AIR 1971 SC 9
(Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India) that the amendment seeks to chang
the composition of the society, he submitted that by admission of ne
members by the general body, there is no increase in the membership o
the society beyond what is fixed under the bye-laws. There is n
alteration in the voluntary character of a member choosing to admit
member to the society. The admission or the rejection of an eligibl
member is always vested with the society, exercised through the genera
body in the absence of a governing body, in that, there is no giving u
of the voluntary character of the society or the democratic functionin
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

or democratic control of a society.
74. The decision reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanti Naranga v
Union of India) is totally distinguishable from the provisions of th
Act and unlike the facts of the reported decision, the presen
amendment does not, in any manner do away with/curtail the rights o
the existing members to admit members. The amendment does not seek t
amend the bye-law to compulsorily bring in members contrary to th
bye-laws of the Society. The amendment merely addresses the mischie
arising on account of the absence of a board in the matter o
admitting new members. In the circumstances, referring to the decisio
reported in AIR 1997 SC 1413 (State of U.P. and another Vs. C.O.D
Chheoki Employees Cooperative Society Ltd.), particularly to paragrap
15, he submitted that what is implicit in the corporate nature of th
society is made statutorily explicit, that in the absence of th
governing body, the general body is the competent and the only body t
admit new members.
75. As regards the submission on the deemed admission, h
submitted that the Second proviso to Section 21(2) of the Act, eve
before the amendment, provided for deemed admission if the Board doe
not consider an application of an eligible member seeking admissio
into the society within sixty days of receipt of the application. I
the petitioners have no grievance over that, equally there could be n
grievance on the amendment made to the second proviso by insertion o
the words as has been done in the main section 21 (2). The allegatio
that the provisions are unreasonable, unworkable and arbitrary are no
substantiated by the petitioners, who are the members of the societ
and it is an argument in vain . In the absence of any material t
substantiate this contention, the presumption as to th
constitutionality of the provision has to be upheld. In any event, i
there be any violation it is always open to the aggrieved party t
seek remedial action and this has nothing to do with th
constitutionality of the provision.
76. As regards the contention that the amending Act has no
received the assent of the President, he submitted that making law a
regards Co-operative Society is a State subject. Being a State subjec
falling under List II, the State is entitled to legislate on the sai
subject matter. Co-operative Societies Act of 1963 was originall
assented to by the President only on account of specific provision
contained therein, making certain Central Acts as not applicable to th
Co-operative Societies. In the circumstances, the amended Act is
regulatory measure and hence, the same cannot be invalidated on th
ground of non-compliance of Article 255 of the Constitution of India.
77. Taking the submissions further, learned Additional Advocat
General referred to the decision reported in JT 2012 (9) SC 209 (Nami
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Sharma Vs. Union of India (UOI) as to the ground on which
constitutionality of a provision can be challenged. He pointed out tha
Article 246(3) empowers the State to legislate on matters falling unde
List II of Seventh Schedule to the Constitution of India. Articl
243ZI directs the State to legislate with respect to the incorporation
regulation, winding up of the Co-operative societies, based on th
principles of voluntary formation, democratic member control, membe
economic participation and autonomous functioning. Referring to th
decision reported in 1978 Kar.L.J. 302 (H.Puttappa & others Vs. Th
State of Karnataka and others), particularly to paragraphs 6 and 11, h
submitted that the present provision is a regulatory measure t
provide for the mechanism for admission of new members to a society
whenever there is no governing body functioning. Hence, the provision
made are in tune with what is spoken to under Article 243ZI of th
Constitution of India.
78. Defending the amendment that it is not in violation of th
Full Bench decision of this Court, he relied on the decision reporte
in AIR 1980 SC 1707 (Rajput Ruda Meha Vs. State of Gujarat) a
paragraph 6. and contended that as far as the Full Bench decision o
this Court is concerned, the judgment is an authority for th
proposition that the Special Officer is an in charge Officer appointe
to take care of the day-to-day affairs of the Society in the absence o
the Board. The question raised therein was on the validity of Sectio
89-A, empowering the Special Officer to admit new members. So too, i
the decision reported (2000) 6 SCC 127 (Joint Registrar of Co-operativ
Societies, Kerala V. T.A.Kuttappan), the Apex Court reiterated the vie
that the Special Officer has no authority to admit new members and i
is only the General Body/Governing Body, who could admit new members
Thus, these decisions are concerned about the authority of the Specia
Officer and the issue raised herein is a totally different issue
Referring to the decision of the Karnataka High Court reported in 199
(1) Kar.L.J. 576 (M.L.Nagaraj Vs. State of Karnataka) at paragraph 15
he submitted that the General Body is the ultimate authority to decide
with whom the society is to deal with and who should be associated wit
it for promoting the object of the Society. Section 21(2) as it stoo
originally gave authority to the Board to decide on admitting ne
members. He further pointed out that the Full Bench judgment of thi
court has not held that the General Body has no authority to admit ne
members. On the other hand, it also recognised the supreme authorit
of the general body to admit new members. Referring to paragraph 49 o
the Full Bench decision, he also referred to the decision of th
Karnataka High Court reported in 1995(1) Kar.L.J. 576 (M.L.Nagaraj Vs
State of Karnataka), particularly to paragraphs 15 and 21, which wa
approved by the Apex Court in the decision reported in (1997) 3 SCC 3
(K.Shantharaj Vs.M.L.Nagaraj) and submitted that the decisions canno
be taken as having concluded the issue as to the authority of th
general body to admit members that too when the amendment merel
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

provides for a situation brought about by the absence of the board
Going by the above, no illegality could be attributed to the amende
provision.
79. In any event, he submitted that the general body is
repository of all powers and as a matter of convenience alone, th
administration is given to the board whose members are elected from ou
of the general body. Thus, the power available with the general bod
is only delegated to the board for the purpose of administration
Hence, one cannot look on the governing body as without any authorit
to admit new members. In this connection, he placed reliance on th
decision reported in AIR 1961 MP 40 (Mishrimal Vs. District Cooperativ
Grower's Association Ltd.), particularly to paragraphs 18 and 19, tha
the board is only the delegatee of the power vested in the Genera
Body, which entrusts its authority on the Governing Body for th
purposes of administering the Society. He also placed reliance on th
decisions reported in AIR 1963 SC 1503 (Roop Chand Vs. State o
Punjab), [2000] 118 STC 140 (J.C.Budharaja Vs. State of Orissa an
others) at paragraph 10, 2006-3-L.W. 546 (L.Ramasubbu an
K.S.A.Krishnamoorthy Vs. Madura College Board, Madura College
Tharapuram Kundram Road, Madurai, Tamil Nadu) at paragraphs 5 and 54
that the general body is a body with supreme authority in the Societ
and even if there be a limitation on its powers, it would only b
subject to such restrictions as would be there in the bye-laws or i
the statute. He also pointed out that there is nothing in the bye-law
to limit the power of the General Body and Section 32 of the Ac
specifically refers to the ultimate authority of the General Body, bu
subject to the provisions of the Act, Rules and Bye-laws. This doe
not mean that the State cannot make law to invest the authority back o
the General Body to admit new members, whereever there is vacuum in th
exercise of a particular authority. He also pointed out that th
authority of the general body on the admission of new members arise
only on account of the absence of the governing body.
80. Learned Additional Advocate General also referred t
provisions similar to Section 21(2) available in the enactments o
other States, namely, Bihar, Goa, Jharkhand and Mizoram. He als
referred to the model Co-operative Societies Act providing for th
exercise of authority by the general body in the absence of th
governing body to admit new members and no mala fide intention can b
attributed to the introduction of the amendment, which merely provide
for a lacuna in the provisions and that too to be discharged in th
absence of the Board. In so providing, there is no violation of any o
the constitutional provisions, including Part IX-B of the Constitutio
of India.
81. On the submission of the petitioners as to the absence o
amendment in the Rule and Form 16 in consonance with Section 21(2) o
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

the Act at the time of admission of members by the General Bod
pursuant to the amendment in Section 21(2) of the Act, he pointed ou
to the decisions reported in 1962 (2) SCR 880 (Seth Bikhraj Jaipuri
Vs. Union of India) at paragraph 16 and 1980 Supp (1) SCC 27 (Ra
Chandra Kumar & Co. Vs. State of U.P.) at paragraphs 35 and 44 an
submitted that the Rules are to sub-serve the substantial provisions o
the Act. Hence, the Rules could never be in conflict with th
provisions of the Act. Even if the Rules are not amended, in so far a
they are not in conflict with the Section, the Rules will nevertheles
apply in any given situation. Thus, even assuming that the Rules ar
not amended, the substantive part of the Rules still having relevanc
to the amended provision, no illegality could be read in to th
amendment brought subsequently to the Rules and the Form, which is th
normal case in every amendment. There is no substantive differenc
between the unamended Form and the amended Form, except for th
insertion of the words "general body in the absence of governing body"
Consequently, the details specified in Form 16 to be furnished by a
intending member remaining the same, no illegality could be read in t
the admission of members on the basis of unamended Form No.16. In thi
connection, he also referred to the decisions reported in (2011) 2 SC
1 (CIT Vs. Tulsyan NEC Ltd.) at paragraph 20, AIR 1975 AP 242 (Mohd
Basha Vs. Secretary, Regional Transport Authority) at paragraph 5 an
1984 Crl.LJ 1259 (Kailas Prosad Musaddi Vs. The State) at paragraph
and submitted that when the Form, as it stood originally, contained al
the particulars necessary for showing the eligibility, the part of th
Form for office use not containing the amendment as per the Section
does not make the Rules and the Forms as in violation of the provision
of the Act.
82. One of the contentions taken in these writ petitions relate
to the mode of serving notice on the members as regards the convenin
of the general body. Learned Additional Advocate General referred t
Rule 46, which provides for more than one method of notice to be give
in calling for the general body meeting. He pointed out that the Rule
do not contemplate paper publication as the last of the modes to b
undertaken after exhausting personal notice on the member. He pointe
out that a reading of Rule 46 would show that it contemplated servic
of notice through one or more of the methods specified therein. Thus
going by the wording in the above-said Rule that one or more method
enumerated therein may be observed for serving notice on the member
and the period of notice as provided for under the byelaws, the notic
given through paper publication cannot be taken exception to. H
further submitted that when the byelaw specified the share capital, th
number of shares to be allotted to the individual member, subject t
the maximum, and the total membership capacity to a society is fixe
therein, the question of admitting new members beyond the membershi
capacity fixed therein in the bye laws does not arise even if th
Governing Body is there to admit new members. The question of changin
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

the composition of the society would arise only when the number o
persons admitted exceed the maximum member capacity fixed under th
bye-laws or an ineligible member admitted to the Society or for tha
matter without the involvement of the members to decide on th
admission of a member, the present amendment does not do away with th
free will of the members to decide on the admission of a person seekin
admission in the society.
83. He pointed out that there are as many as 22,000 Co-operativ
Societies in the State. The petitioners before this Court are member
of nearly 134 Co-operative Societies. None of the Societies functio
with full capacity of membership as stated in the bye-laws. As such
admission of new members by the general body is well within th
framework of each of the societies on the maximum number of admitte
members and the contention of the petitioners, particularly Mr.Wilson
learned senior counsel appearing for the petitioners in W.P.Nos.2569
25882, 25889 and 25890 of 2012 and Mr.R.Shanmugasundaram learned senio
counsel appearing for the petitioners in W.P.Nos.31749 of 2012, 112
and 1127 of 2013, that their society is functioning in full capacity i
incorrect and not borne out by records.
 84. In this connection, he produced the data showing the presen
membership strength of the Societies, to which the petitioners belong
When the amendment does not contemplate statutory admission of members
thereby interfering with the choice and option of the existing member
to admit or reject the application for membership to the Society, th
reliance placed on the decision reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyant
Naranga v. Union of India) that the admission of new members by th
General Body would change the character of the society is incorrect an
not substantiated by facts. He also relied on the observation
particularly in pages 967, 968, 969, 970 and 971 in the above-sai
decision to his aid that there is no unconstitutionality in th
amendment made to Section 21(2) of the Act. Referring to the decisio
reported in (1984) 2 SCC 50 (Babaji Kondaji Garad Vs. Nasik) that
byelaw must go in tune with the provisions of the Act and the Rules, h
submitted that given the supreme authority of the General Body, in th
absence of specific express provision doing away with the authority o
the General Body under the byelaw or the statute, the inheren
authority of the General Body to admit members in the absence of th
Governing Body cannot be overlooked or denied. Thus, no exceptio
could be taken to the admission of new members by the general body
more so when there is no governing body existing to admit new members
The said admission of new members would be in consonance with th
democratic functioning of the society. Hence, the provision does no
offend Article 19(1)(c) or Article 14, or for that matter, Part IX-B o
the Constitution of India; consequently, there are no merits in th
submissions of the petitioners herein.
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

85. We have bestowed our attention to the arguments raised o
either side. We agree with the contention of the State on th
constitutionality of the amending Act. We have already noted that th
Constitution (Ninety Seventh) Amendment Act, 2011 inserted the right t
form a Co-operative Society, a fundamental right guaranteed unde
Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution of India and the said right may b
subjected to reasonable restriction if it is so warranted in th
interest of the sovereignty and integrity of India, public order o
morality.
86. Article 19(1)(c) speaks about the right to form a co-operativ
society as a fundamental right. Article 43-B -Directive principle o
State policy, touches on the responsibilities of the State to promot
voluntary formation, autonomous functioning, democratic control an
professional management of the cooperative societies. Article 243-Z
enjoins on the State, subject to the provisions of Part IX-B, to mak
provisions with reference to the incorporation, regulation and windin
up of the cooperative societies based on the principles of voluntar
formation, democratic member control, member economic participation an
autonomous functioning. The provisions subject to which the State ma
make laws, relate to number and term of members of Board of Director
or the governing body (Article 243ZJ), election to the Board (Articl
243ZK), supersession and suspension of Board and interim management an
its duration (Article 243ZL); Penalties to be provided for in th
State Act (Article 243ZQ). Where any of the provisions of the Stat
statute are inconsistent with the provisions of the Central Act
Article 243ZT stipulates that the State shall amend or repeal the sam
within one year from the commencement of the provision. As regards th
audit of accounts and the convening of general body, the State i
directed to make provisions regulating the above-said subjects. Thu
the Cooperative Societies Acts of the States are to provide for matter
specified in the Constitution and in accordance and consistent wit
what is stated in Part IX-B of the Constitution of India. At the sam
time, the Constitution also directs the States to legislate on matter
which are regulatory as to the formation, regulation of the conduct o
business and affairs of the Society on the principles of voluntar
formation, democratic member control, member economic participation
autonomous functioning and professional management.
87. A reading of Article 243-ZJ "number and term of members of th
Board and its office bearers", on which much reliance is placed by th
petitioners in support of the argument as indicative of the power an
expertise of the Board alone to decide on the admission of new members
reveals that while prescribing the maximum number of directors in th
Co-operative Society as not more than 21, it leaves it to the State t
prescribe the number of directors and it enjoins on the State t
provide for reservation of one seat for SC/ST and two seats for wome
on board of every Society. The term of office of directors is fixe
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

under sub-article (2) as five years from the date of election and tha
the term of the office bearer shall be co-terminous with the term o
the board. In the event of a casual vacancy arising and the term o
office of the board is less than half of the original term, the boar
may fill the vacancy by nomination out of the same class of members i
respect of which the casual vacancy has arisen. The said Article als
provides for the State legislature to make provision for co-option o
members, having experience in the field of banking management
financial or specialisation in any other field relating to the object
and activities undertaken by the co-operative Society, to be members o
the Society. This shall be in addition to the 21 directors and th
number of co-opted members shall not exceed two in number, who shal
not have the right to vote in the election of the Society or to b
eligible for election as office bearers of the board. It also make
the functional directors as members of the board. Thus, apart fro
fixing the maximum number of members in the board and its duration, th
above Article makes the board a representative board in consonanc
with the constitutional mandate on reservation, providing fo
professional management by co-opting two experts as members in th
board.
88. Thus, the prescription in Article 243-ZJ covers only thos
matters referred to above and that the State has no legislativ
competence to regulate the functioning or the authority of the Board
The fact that it provides for professional management by co-optin
members in the Board does not mean that the professional expertise i
for admission of members It is no doubt true that Article 243ZH(b
defines "Board" to mean the Board of Directors or the governing body o
a cooperative society by whatever name called, to which the directio
and 'control of the management of the affairs of a society is entruste
to'. The use of the word "entrusted to" in the definition clearl
points out that whatever control and authority that the Board has, ar
all what is entrusted to the Board for the management of the affairs o
the society and nothing beyond and that there exists an authority ove
and above the Board to entrust the said authority to the board. Thus
as already pointed out earlier, with the responsibility thrust on th
State to make provisions with respect to incorporation, regulation
etc., of the cooperative societies, one cannot read any limiting of th
authority in the State in making provisions on the extent of authorit
to be entrusted to the Board to direct and control/manage the affair
of the Society. This authority to legislate thus extends to a wid
range of topics relating to the affairs of the Society, over which th
Board would exercise control and it also included such incidental an
ancillary matters relating to the authority and functioning of th
Board as well as regulating the functioning of the Society. In thi
connection, it may be seen that Article 243–ZL provides on the subjec
of supersession and suspension of the Board and the interim managemen
of the affairs of the Board. It provides for the grounds on which th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Board may be suspended or kept under suspension and the period upt
which the Board may be suspended or superseded. Sub-Article (2) state
that the Administrator appointed to manage the affairs of such societ
shall arrange for the conduct of election within the period of si
months or as provided in the proviso to Sub-Article (1) and hand ove
the management to the elected Board. Thus, the authority of the Stat
is provided for under Article 243-ZI. Given the settled proposition o
law that the Administrator has no authority to admit new members an
his authority is limited to the managing of the affairs of the societ
alone during the supersession or suspension of the Board and that th
Legislature is assumed to know this law, we do not find any limiting o
the authority of the State Legislature to make such provisions as ar
required to meet those situations caused by the suspension/supersessio
of the Board and the limitation on the powers of the Administrato
consistent with the principles of voluntary formation and democrati
control, to promote the autonomous functioning of the society. Thus
when the amending Act filled up the lacuna as regards the admission o
new members during the time when the Board is in suspension b
entrusting the same with the General Body, we find, there is nothin
inconsistent or violative of any of the provisions conferred under Par
IX-B of the Constitution of India. The Tamil Nadu Amending Act thu
provides for the regulatory measure to ensure that the autonomou
functioning and member participation promoting the co-operativ
movement does not suffer any casualty on account of suspension of th
management body, namely, the Board.
89. In this context, the right guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c
of the Constitution of India needs to be seen. There is no denial o
the fact that there is no compulsion to form or become a member of
society under the Constitution. An individual is free to form o
become a member of the society and the Constitution recognises thi
right as a fundamental right under Article 19(1)(c).
90. Thus, in the background of such a right, when a group o
citizens volunteer to form a co-operative society, Section 4 of the Ac
states that a society, which has its objects of promoting the economi
interests or general welfare of its members in accordance with co
operative principles, may be registered under the Act with limited o
unlimited liability. Registration of a Society under the Act confer
certain benefits and privileges on the Society and the members unde
the Act and the Rules formed thereunder. Thus, on the formation of th
Society, the interest of the members merges with the interest of th
Society and there is no such thing as an individual member's interest
qua the society. In the decision reported in AIR 1985 SC 973 (Dama
Singh v. State of Punjab), the Apex Court pointed out "once a perso
becomes a member of a cooperative society, he loses his individualit
qua the society and he has no independent rights except those given t
him by the statute and the byelaws. He must act and speak through th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

society or rather the society alone can act and speak for him qu
rights or duties of the society as a body. Pointing out to Section 3
of the Punjab Cooperative Societies Act, which is similar to Section 3
of the Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies Act, the Supreme Court observe
that the registration of the cooperative society renders it a bod
corporate by the name under which it is registered, having perpetua
succession and a common seal and with power to hold property, ente
into contract, institute and defend suits and other legal proceeding
and to do all things necessary for the purpose for which it i
constituted. Thus "there cannot, therefore be a slightest doubt that
cooperative society is a corporation as commonly understood."
91. In the decision reported in AIR 1976 AP 340 (Kaamaredd
Suryanarayana and Anr. Vs. The District Co-operative Officer-cum
Election Officer, The West Godavari Co-operative Sugars Ltd. and Ors
the Andhra Pradesh High Court observed in paragraph 22 "If a Society i
formed and is registered under the Act and its members claim specia
privileges and advantages conferred by the Act and the Rules frame
thereunder, they cannot turn round and say that they are not bound b
any restrictions imposed by some provision of the Act or the Rules i
the interests of good and fair administration and working of th
society on the ground that they are violative of the fundamental righ
to form Associations guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c) of th
Constitution and the restrictions imposed are not those contemplate
under clause (4) of Article 19 and therefore they are not bound b
them. "
92. The petitioners, however, contend that the right guarantee
under Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution is not confined to th
initial stage of forming the association, but it protects the right t
continue with the members forming the society to carry on the societ
in the manner they desire. Any law passed interfering with this righ
would be hit by Article 19(1)(c), unless saved by Article 19(4) of th
Constitution of India. Thus, the person forming the association ha
the right under Article 19(1)(c) to see that the composition of th
society continues as voluntarily agreed to by them. In thi
connection, the petitioners lay great stress on the decision of th
Apex Court reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanti Naranga v. Union o
India), followed by the decision reported in (2000) 6 SCC 127 (Join
Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V. T.A.Kuttappan) an
(2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co-operative Housing Society Ltd. V
District Registrar, Co-operative (Urban) and Others).
93. As far as this contention is concerned, the State does no
dispute, in principle, that the right guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c
includes the right of the members to continue the association a
voluntarily agreed upon by the members and the same cannot, in an
manner, be curtailed or interfered with, except in the manner stated i
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Article 19(4) of the Constitution of India. But the State contend
that the amendment has nothing to do with imposing of any restrictio
on the right of voluntariness to admit new members; hence, th
decisions are not of any assistance to the petitioners.
94. The decisions relied on by the petitioners have to be seen i
the background of the provisions which came up for challenge in th
decisions, the first which is the one reported in AIR 1971 SC 96
(Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India). The challenge therein was as t
the validity of the Central Act. Under Section 4(4) of the Act, a
retrospectively amended in the year 1963, apart from persons who wer
members of the society, there were admission of new members into th
society without any option being available to the original members o
the society to admit or refuse admission to them. The Apex Cour
observed that the right to form an association implied that the person
forming the association have also the right to continue to b
associated with those whom they voluntarily admit in the Association
Thus, any law by which members are brought into a voluntary associatio
without any option to the members to decide on the admission or kee
them out or any law which takes away the option, will be a la
violating the right to form an association under Article 19(1)(c) o
the Constitution of India.
95. On considering Sections 4 and 12(1)(a), the Supreme Cour
pointed out that when new members were added without any option bein
available to the existing members of the Society to elect or refuse t
elect them as members, which was the right they possessed under th
constitution of the society itself, there was "a clear interferenc
with the right to form an association which had been exercised by th
members of the society by forming the society with its constitutio
under which they were members and future members could only come in a
a result of their choice by being elected by their working committee
Thus the Apex Court called the admission of the new members without th
right of the old members to consider the admission, as interfering wit
the composition of the society and hence, hit by Article 19(1)(c) o
the Constitution of India.
96. The above-said law laid down by the Supreme Court wa
reiterated in the decision reported in AIR 1989 SC 2126 (Aso
Rastrabhasa Prachar Samiti and Anr. vs. State of Assam and Ors.
wherein the Apex Court observed "Any law by which members ar
introduced in the voluntary association without any option being give
to the members to keep them out; or any law which takes away th
membership of those who have voluntarily formed it, will be a la
violating the right to form an association." The decision was agai
reiterated in (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co-operative Housin
Society Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operative (Urban) and Others).
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

97. The decision of the Apex Court reported in AIR 1971 SC 96
(Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India) and AIR 1989 SC 2126 (Aso
Rastrabhasa Prachar Samiti and Anr. vs. State of Assam and Ors.) cam
up for consideration in the decision reported in 2007 97 DRJ 273 (Raji
Mukhopadhyaya and Ors. Vs. Registrar Cooperative Societies). Referrin
to the said decision, the Delhi High Court referred to the decisio
reported in 1985 (3) SCR 580 Daman Singh Case and pointed out that th
principle of voluntary and open membership would exclude the State
interference in the affairs of the society. If the society has an
requirement in the byelaws about the characteristics required to b
possessed by an intending member, it has the right to restrict it
membership in accordance with the byelaw of the society. Thus, th
fundamental principle on which every cooperative society functions i
voluntary and open membership and democratic control. Thus, whe
forming of a cooperative society is the result of free will of member
joining to form a Society and likewise, when a society admits a perso
into the membership, the voluntariness with which they joined to for
an association would continue to decide on who should be assoicate
with them in their common object. The Delhi High Court referred t
the decision reported in 1988 CLJ 75 (Navjivan Cooperative Hous
Building Society v. Delhi Cooperative Tribunal, Delhi), wherein, si
principles on cooperation are enumerated, which read as under:
" (i) Voluntary and open membership;
(ii)Democratic control;
(iii)Limited interest on capital;
(iv)Equitable division of surplus;
(v)Co-operative Education;
(vi)Co-operation among cooperatives.
85. At page 82 of the judgment it was observed by the learned
single Judge in Navjivan Cooperative Society's case, while
referring to the principle of voluntary and open membership as
follows:
The principle that the membership shall be voluntary in
the Cooperative Society means that:
(i) a person who joins a cooperative society of
his own free will, and
(ii) the society which admits a person into its
membership should likewise do so voluntarily.
The principle of open membership is that:
(i) there shall be no artificial restriction on
the admission of members;
(ii) there shall be no social, political,
racial or religious discrimination against
persons who wish to join, and
(iii) memberships shall be available to all
persons who need and can make use of the
society's services and are willing to accept
the responsibilities of membership.
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

The principle of voluntary, open membership would
naturally exclude the state from becoming a member of a
Cooperative Society and would exclude the state
interference in the affairs of the society.
98. The Delhi High Court observed "It is always like minde
persons who join together and form a cooperative society. If member
are thrust on the society then the spirit of cooperation an
comradeship will come to an end. " There can be no two views on thi
and we respectfully agree with what has been stated therein in th
above decision.
99. The case of the petitioners is that the amendment to Sectio
21(2) by investing on the general body, the authority to admit ne
members in the absence of the Board, is violative of Article 19(1)(c
and that by the said amendment, the entry of the new members admitte
by the general body would be against the bye law of the Society an
will interfere with the composition of the Society. Thus, th
compulsory thrust of the members interferes with the fundamental righ
guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c) and against the law declared in th
decisions reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanti Naranga v. Union o
India) and (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co-operative Housing Societ
Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operative (Urban) and Others) and o
that ground, the Section has to be struck down. We do not find an
good ground to accept this contention.
100. As already seen, while prescribing who shall be eligibl
for admission as a member, Section 21(1) of the Act further provide
that a registered society may specify such further qualification as ma
be specified in the Rules or byelaws. Having thus provided for th
qualification required for admission as a member, the Act left it t
the registered society to decide on the admission of associate membe
(Section 22). Thus, as far as the admission of a member other than a
associate member is concerned, Section 21(2), as it originally stood
conferred authority on the Board to admit new members. However, as t
the expulsion of a member suffering disqualification as per Section 25
it is the General Body, which is vested with the authority to expel th
members through a resolution at a special meeting.
101. Chapter IV of the Act deals with the management of registere
societies. Section 32 states that subject to the provisions of th
Act, the Rules and the byelaws, the ultimate authority of a registere
society shall vest in the general body of its members. The provisio
qualifies the ultimate authority of the general body by stating tha
such ultimate authority of the general body shall not affect th
exercise of any power by the board or any officer of the registere
society conferred on such board / such officer by the Act or Rules o
the byelaws. Sub section (2) deals with the convening of the genera
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

body for considering the subjects stated therein. Section 33 deal
with the constitution and meeting of the Board. It states that th
management of every society shall vest in a board constituted i
accordance with the provisions of the Act, Rules and the byelaws, whic
shall exercise such power and perform such duties as may be conferre
or imposed in it by the Act, Rules and the byelaws. Given th
constitution of the Board to represent the society through member
elected by the General Body, it represents the collective will of th
members. Thus, the status of the Board is more in the nature of a
agent and trustee to represent the Society to discharge such powers a
are conferred under the Act, Rules and the Byelaws.
102. Thus, one such authority given to the Board under the Ac
relates to admission of new member. Section 21(2), as it originall
stood, conferred authority on the Board to admit new members, who ar
eligible for admission, as members of a society under the provisions o
the Act, the Rules and the byelaws. Having thus given the authority t
admit new members, it also gave the authority to the Board to rejec
the application for good and sufficient reasons recorded in the minute
of the meeting, thereby refuse admission to any individual. Suc
reservation of power to admit or reject the application for membershi
is well in accordance with the principle, that membership shall b
voluntary in the co-operative society and the society which admits
person into its membership, likewise acts voluntarily to admit o
reject the application seeking membership. As held in the decision o
the Apex Court reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanti Naranga v. Unio
of India), the right guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c) is not onl
limited to the initial stage of forming the association, but extend
further to include within it, the right to continue the associatio
with its composition as voluntarily agreed upon by the persons formin
the association. Given the right to form an association or cooperativ
society, a constitutional right under Article 19(1)(c), the right t
become a member of a society is a matter of individual free will, bu
is governed by the provisions of the Act, Rules and the byelaws. Thus
when a society admits an eligible person as a member, it does so out o
its free will and voluntarily. Given the discretion to the Board t
admit an eligible person as a member, the society exercises democrati
control in choosing who from among the eligible applicants should b
admitted to associate as a member of the society. With all th
authority given to the Board to admit the members, in the matter o
expulsion of a member, it is not the board but the general body alon
which is given the authority to pass a resolution in this regard. Thus
as already noted, admittedly, the absence of the Board for merely te
years has thus brought in a stalemate in the matter of admission of ne
members. We do not think that such a stalemate advances the cause o
co-operative movement or in the interests of the co-operative movemen
to grow further in achieving the very object for which the society i
formed. In the background of this situation brought about by th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

absence of the Board, the present amendment to Section 21(2) is brough
to fill up this gap that in the absence of the Board, the general bod
is the competent body to admit new members. In so conferring such a
authority, we do not find any interference with the byelaws of th
society, for, even when the byelaws provided for admission of ne
members by the Board, it is only by virtue of Section 21(2) and not b
reason of the individual society's decision. A reading of the byelaw
of the petitioners produced before this Court shows that apart fro
prescribing certain eligibility criteria, which are specific to th
objects of the society, matters concerning the eligibility
disqualification, transfer of shares, composition of the Board an
admission of new members are all taken only from the Act. In th
circumstances, we fail to understand how any particular petitione
society could claim that providing for admission of new members by th
Board is a matter provided for under the byelaw alone and hence, canno
be tinkered with by the statute.
103. Section 33 specifically states that the Board shall exercis
such powers and perform such duties as may be conferred and imposed b
the Act, Rules and byelaws. Hence, what is prescribed by the statute
Rules and the bye laws alone govern the authority of the board. A
already seen, the byelaws of the society must be in conformity with th
Acts and the Rules. Rule 6 provides the areas on which the byelaws o
the society may provide for. Thus, although sub clause (g) of sub rul
(1) of Rule 6 provides that the byelaw of the society may provide fo
the constitution of the Board and the duties and powers of the Board
when the byelaw provides for admission of members by the Board, it i
not as though the Board derives its power from the byelaw, for, even i
the byelaw does not provide for it, the Act makes provisions for suc
authority to be exercised by the Board. In the circumstances, n
petitioner society can claim that providing for a mechanism o
admission of new member is a matter entirely at the exclusiv
authority of the society provided for under the byelaws. It may als
be noted that none of the byelaws produced before us go against th
provisions of Section 21(2), to contend that it is open to the societ
to prescribe an authority other than the Board to admit new members
If the Act can provide for a mechanism, namely, the Board, to admit ne
member in consonance with the principles of cooperation and democrati
control, it is equally open to the State to provide for a situatio
caused by the absence of a Board to admit new members on the very sam
principles of free will and democratic control by entrusting tha
authority on the general body.
104. Thus, in providing for such a situation, there is n
illegality or arbitrariness or unconstitutionality to interfere wit
the right guaranteed under Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution
Hence, we hold that the amended provisions are constitutional and intr
vires.
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

105. We further hold that interference with the democratic contro
of a society on the admission of new members would arise only if th
State seeks to interfere with the eligibility criteria, as provided fo
in the byelaws, to admit ineligible members or admit members more tha
what the society could hold beyond what is prescribed in the byelaws
or where the State interferes with or controls the discretion of th
society to admit new members against the will of the society o
restricts the admission of members into the society. So long as th
amending Act does not interfere with the discretion and free will o
the general body on its decision to admit new members, no fault coul
be alleged on the amending Act, giving the authority to the genera
body, that too in the absence of the Board only, as uncontitutional
The amendment that has been brought in under Section 21(2) thus fill
up the gap or a situation caused by the absence of the Board. Thus th
decision of the Apex Court reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyant
Naranga v. Union of India) has no bearing on the issue. Th
observation made therein as regards the admission of new member
altering the composition of the society has to be understood in th
context of the provisions of the impugned Act that original members
who voluntarily formed the association, were compelled to act in tha
Association with other members who were brought in as members by tha
Act without any option given to the original members to decide on thei
admission and in whose admission to membership, the original member
had no say.
106. The crux of the Supreme Court decision is "the right to for
an association, in our opinion, necessarily implies that the person
forming the association have also the right to continue to b
associated with only those whom they voluntarily admit in th
Association. Any law by which members are introduced in the voluntar
Association without any option being given to the members to keep the
out, or any law which takes away the membership of those who hav
voluntarily joined it, will be a law violating the right to form a
association." Thus, autonomous functioning and democratic control i
prescribing the eligibility of person to be admitted into a societ
extends to the admission of persons according to the will of th
society and voluntarily. The fact that the general body is a large
body than the Board, by itself, cannot lead to an argument that th
general body would be less autonomous in its activities or that it wil
lose sight of the democratic control that needs to be exercised in th
matter of admission of new members. Thus the exercise of autonomy an
democratic control does not depend on the size or the number of th
body, but rests on the society at large, whose interests alone th
Board represents and nothing more. The autonomous functioning an
democratic control are still preserved and the amendment does not, i
any manner, dilute these principles which are the hallmarks of th
cooperative society.
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

107. The petitioners, however, strongly contended that the Boar
is the specialised body in the matter of admission of new members. I
is contended that a mere eligibility, does not, per se, ensur
admission to a society. Endowed with wisdom and better decision makin
capacity, the Board alone could decide on who, among the eligibl
applicants, could be admitted. If the authority is vested with th
General body which is comparatively larger in capacity, the possibilit
of unmerited persons entering the society would always be there; th
general body may not have the wisdom of the Board to assess th
individual merits of an application, that the admission of suc
undesirable member would alter the composition of the society. Th
petitioners further contended that with the authority vested in th
general body, there would be influx of members admitted through deeme
membership which amounts to back-door entry, which would not b
conducive to the healthy growth of the society, apart from it bein
detrimental to the democratic control. Referring to the decisio
reported in 2006 (1) CTC 1 (FB) (K.Nithyanandam Vs. State of Tami
Nadu), the petitioners contended that the admission of members is th
primary right of the society; enrolment of members changes the ver
composition of the society; the admission of new member on deliberatio
as regards the eligibility to be a member, is entirely the discretio
of the Board of Directors elected by the General Body. The petitione
made particular emphasis on the observation of this Court in paragraph
68 and 69, which read as under:
" 68. At this juncture, it is to be pointed out that the
observation made by the Division Bench relating to
the same on 21.12.1995 is prior to the Kuttappan's
case (A.I.R.2000 S.C.2378). Further, in the said
decision of the Division Bench, the same was not
the main issue and the said observation was only
incidental in the light of the then prevailing
circumstances. As a matter of fact, it was only an
obiter. In any event, subsequent to the law
declared by the Supreme Court in Kuttappan's case,
the observation made by the Division Bench as
referred to above, has become non-est.
69. In view of the above discussion, this Court is of
the view that the Co-operative Societies are
constituted to function through the democratic
management by the General Body through its elected
board which alone is empowered to enrol the new
members. If the power of enrolment of new members
is conferred on the Special Officer who is
appointed for a limited period with limited power
to manage the affairs of the society till the
constitution of newly elected board,it would amount
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

to conferring powers on the Special Officer for
alteration of the composition of the society
itself, which affects not only the democratic set
up and management of the society, but also the
scheme of the Act itself. "
Thus, according to the petitioner, these are all matters which ar
properly left to the discretion of the elected board, which, due to it
intimacy with men and matters of the locality, would be well- equippe
to assess the qualifications and disqualifications of an applicant.
108. Thus according to the petitioners, the provision is a
unreasonable restriction on the democratic control and autonomy of th
society not saved by Article 19(4) of the Constitution of India
Induction of such members would only make the members of such society
minority and hence, the law laid down in the decision reported in AI
1971 SC 966 (Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India) would govern the case
109. The State, on its part, relied on as to the observation i
paragraph 49 of the said judgment and submitted that the Full Bench o
this Court as well as the decision of the Apex Court reiterated tha
the right of the Society to admit members vests with the general bod
or the Board. This Court has not, in any manner, held that the genera
body has no authority to admit new members. All that the decision o
this Court held was that Section 89A conferring the right on th
Special Officer to admit new members, is unconstitutional, that th
rights of the society, the discretion and independence of the member
in the matter of admission of new members cannot, in any manner, b
taken away or whittled down by the State by empowering an authorit
appointed to take care of the day-to-day functioning of the Society
Thus, when the rights of the members to choose the members is take
away by the authority vested on the Special Officer, this Court hel
that Section 89A offended Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitution o
India. Thus, the decision of this Court reported in 2006 (1) CTC
(K.Nithyanandam Vs. State of Tamil Nadu) was concerned about th
validity of Article 89A and the authority of the Special Officer in th
context of Section 21(2), as it then stood. Hence, the decision o
this Court cannot be read as laying down the law that the general bod
has no authority at all to admit new members.
110. We agree with the submissions of learned Advocate General an
learned Additional Advocate General.
111. It is not disputed that as per Section 33, the Board is a
elected body, the members themselves being the members of the societ
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

and elected by the geneal body. A reading of Section 33 shows that i
provides for the nature and composition of the Board, givin
representation to women and members of the weaker sections of th
society, Scheduled Caste and Scheduled Tribe, in deference to th
