constitutional mandate. It also provides for professional management
which fits in with the professional management contemplated unde
Articles 43B and 243ZJ. The professional management spoken to therei
is for the purpose of conduct of the business and such members have n
right to participate in the election of the society or vote in th
meeting.
112. Thus the composition of the Board is the reflection of th
composition of the general body and is a representative of th
interests of the society in all matters. Thus, if one can repos
confidence in the capacity of members of the general body to elec
members of the Board, it is totally illogical to think otherwise tha
the general body does not possess the competency or expertise to judg
an applicant's merit so as to admit a member in the society. It mus
be kept in mind that the constitution of a cooperative society is mad
up of like-minded members who have joined together to form a societ
with a common object or purpose. For the purpose of achieving th
object, for better conduct of business of the society which is
corporate body, powers are entrusted to a Board elected by the genera
body. Section 39 states "the registration of a society shall rende
it a body corporate by the name under which it is registered, wit
perpetual succession and a common seal and with power to hold property
to enter into contracts, to institute and defend suits and other lega
proceedings and to do all things necessary for the purposes for whic
it is constituted."
113. In the background of the above provisions of the Act and th
powers of the Board which are exercised on matters specified under th
byelaws and the statute, one can only say that it is a representativ
body of the larger body, to represent the interests of the society a
large. It performs such duties as are conferred under the Act, Rule
and the byelaws. Hence, the wisdom with which the Board acts, is th
reflection of the collective wisdom of the general body and so too th
collective wisdom of the general body reflected in the collectiv
wisdom of the board. We do not find there exists any reason or fact
to attribute and recognise greater wisdom and ability in the Board tha
the general body in the matter of appointment of new members. It mus
be remembered that while forming the society is the result of th
consensus born out of free will, the members associate with each othe
with a common purpose to be achieved. Thus, the Board is only an entit
created out of the larger body to represent the larger body of th
society consisting of the general body. Thus, the Board is only
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

convenient agency appointed to transact the business of the society o
behalf of the society. Thus, unlike a Special Officer appointed onl
for the purpose of carrying on the day-to-day affairs of the society
till such time the elected body is appointed, the Board does not an
cannot have powers larger than that of the general body which create
it and it derives its strength only from the larger body. It is to
pretentious to think otherwise that the Board alone has the competenc
and the ability to decide on the admission of new members. Thus, al
that the amendment seeks to achieve is to regulate the admission of ne
members from among the eligible members through the general bod
whenever the Board is not there. This is only a temporary measure til
such time that the board is elected. Unlike the experience of the pas
11 years where no elections were held, after the 97
th amendment, in th
context of Articles 243ZK and Article 243-ZL mandating the holding o
the election to be before the expiry of the term of the board an
suspension of the board for not more than six months, the presen
amended provision empowering the general body to admit new members i
only a transitory provision. However, this aspect is not the onl
ground to hold that the provision is valid. As already pointed out
the capacity of the general body cannot, in any manner, be undermine
to say that the Board alone has the expertise to admit new members. Th
need for observing transparency, deliberation and application of min
in the admission of new members are not qualities that the genera
body may have to search for , for, the scales that the general bod
possesses while electing mmbers of theboard is not less onerous tha
that the general body may have to have while admitting new members
Given the fact that the general body alone is the only authority t
elect the board , the authority given to the Board in admitting ne
members could, only be regarded as the delegation of authority of th
general body to the Board to appoint new members. Given the fact tha
the general body is the ultimate authority in a society Section 2
could only be regarded as the regulatory provision in the matter o
admitting members, there could be no clash of interests between th
board and the general body, that the board shall be recognise
statutorily as having the authority to admit new members It is als
relevant to note that Section 33 itself states that the Board shal
have such powers and perform such duties as may be conferred on o
imposed by the Act, Rules and the byelaws, that under Section 25, a
expulsion of a member of a registered society acting adversely to th
interests of the society could be only through a resolution of th
general body passed at a special meeting convened for the purpose, b
the votes of not less than two-thirds of the total members present an
voting at the meeting. The proviso to Section 25 states that for this
no such special meeting shall be called by the Board except upon th
requisition in writing by not less than one fourth of the total numbe
of members of the society or 25 members, whichever is less. In th
background of the above facts, it is difficult to treat the Boar
alone as the only supreme body having the capacity to admit members
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Thus, if the State has the authority to regulate the admission of ne
members through one forum in a society, it has equally the authority t
empower the only other forum in the society, in the event of the firs
empowered forum lying dormant or suffering incapacity to act in term
of the provisions of the Act, Rules and the byelaws. Given the fac
that the general body alone is the one and only forum in the society i
the place of the Board to admit members, rightly, the amendment fille
up the vacuum, so that admission of new members to the society does no
suffer. In so providing, we do not find any violation of Article 19(1
(c) or any unreasonableness to attract Article 14 of the Constitutio
of India. It is no doubt true that admission of a new member require
adjudication on the merits of the candidature of eligible perso
seeking admission. Thus, when the amended provision provides fo
admission of new members by the general body in the absence of th
Board, in the same manner as has been done by the Board, one canno
visualise the admission by the general body as less voluntary
irrational or without application of mind. Thus, when the amende
provision keeps intact the authority of the society to admit person
into its membership voluntarily with even option to either admit o
reject the application, we do not find anything unconstitutional o
unreasonable in the amended provision to hold that the amende
provision does not protect the rights of the society/ members t
continue the association with the membership chosen voluntarily t
retain its character and composition.
114. This is in accordance with what is laid down as law in th
decisions of the Apex Court reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanthi V
Union of India), AIR 1989 SC 2126 (Asom Rastrabhasa Prachar Samiti an
Anr. vs. State of Assam and Ors.) and (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co
operative Housing Society Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operativ
(Urban) and Others). The decision reported in AIR 1971 SC 9
(Damyanthi V. Union of India) and (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastrian Co
operative Housing Society Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operativ
(Urban) and Others) are all related to cases on admission of member
without any option to the members to exercise their will as in th
decision reported in AIR 1971 SC 966 (Damyanthi V. Union of India) an
on the admission of members who are ineligible to become members in th
society as in the decision reported in (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastria
Co-operative Housing Society Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operativ
(Urban) and Others). Thus the circumstances and facts in these cases
at any cost, cannot be overlooked to, while looking at the reason
arrived at to declare the law therein to these cases a
unconstitutional. In any event, as already pointed out, the amende
provision respects the law declared in maintaining the free will of th
members in selecting the members. Equally, we do not find anything i
the Full Bench decision of this Court being violated by the amendmen
to Section 21(2) of the Act. On the other hand, paragraph 49 clearl
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

pointed out that in a democratic set up, it is the genera
body/executive committee, which would alone have the power to admit ne
members who satisfy the required criteria as per the byelaws of th
society.
115. The decisions of the Supreme Court reported in (2000) 6 SC
127 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V
T.A.Kuttappan) and (1997) 3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj V. M.L.Nagaraj), o
which much reliance was placed by the petitioners, relate to case
relating to the authority of an Administrator appointed during th
suspension of a Management Committee of the cooperative society t
enrol new members. In paragraph 3 of the judgment reported in (2000)
SCC 127 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V
T.A.Kuttappan), the Supreme Court referred to the decision reported i
(1997) 3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj V. M.L.Nagaraj) as follows:
" When an identical question came up before this
Court for consideration in K.Shantharaj case this
Court held that from the language of Section 30
[which is similar to Section 32(4) of the Act] and
Section 30-A of the Karnataka Cooperative Societies
Act, 1959, it would be clear that the
administrator, subject to control of Registrar
exercise all or any of the functions of the
society, and the Special Officer, subject to
control of the State Government and the Registrar
exercise and perform all the powers and functions
of the committee of the society and in the interest
of the society can take such action as is necessary
for proper functioning of the society as per law.
He should conduct elections as is enjoined
thereunder, that is, he is to conduct election with
the members as on the rolls and by necessary
implication, he is not vested with the power to
enrol new members of the society. "
116. Analysing Section 32 of the Kerala Cooperative Societies Act
the Apex Court pointed out:
" If we carefully analyse the provisions of the
Act, it would be clear that the administrator or a
Committee appointed while the Committee of
Management of the Society is under supersession
cannot have the power to enrol new members and such
a question ought not to be decided merely by
indulging in an exercise on semantics in
ascertaining the meaning of the expression have
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

"power to exercise all or any of the functions.".
117. The Apex Court held "a Cooperative Society is expected t
function in a democratic manner through an elected Committee o
Management and that Committee of Management is empowered to enrol ne
members. Enrolment of new members would involve alteration of th
composition of the society itself and such a power should be exercise
by an elected Committee rather than by an administrator or a Committe
appointed by the Registrar while the Committee of Management is unde
supersession." The Apex Court thus reaffirmed the view taken in th
decision reported in (1997) 3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj V. M.L.Nagaraj)
Both these decisions, as in Nithyanandam's case, relate primarily as t
the jurisdiction of the Special Officer to admit new members. Unde
the Kerala Act, Section 32 of the Kerala Co-operative Societies Act
1969 is the provision regarding the admission of the new members by th
society. So too under the Karnataka Act (Section 16), there is n
specific mentioning of either the general body or the board a
entrusted with the jurisdiction to admit new members as we have in th
Tamil Nadu Act. In any event, the question that arose before the Ape
Court was as regards the authority of the Special Officer and the sai
decisions are not concerned on the subject that we have before us. Th
observation of the Apex Court on the alteration of composition of th
society has to be understood in the context of admission of members b
the State in that case without any option to the existing members
Thus, the admission of new members within the broad framework of th
Society in accordance with the provisions of the Act, Rules an
byelaws, which itself has to be in accordance with the Act and th
Rules, does change the composition of the society, except making th
society numericaly stronger. Thus, admission of new members, be it b
the Board or the General Body in the absence of the Board, certainl
would alter the composition of the Society in improving the financia
strengh of the society, improved activity and better quality in th
business of the society. If such alteration brought about by the Boar
by the admission of members is to be viewed as well within the fou
corners of the provisions of the Act, Rules and Bye-laws, we do no
understand, how the authority of the General Body in admitting ne
members in accordance with the provisions of the Act, Rules and th
Bye-laws, would make the composition different from the one brough
about by the Board. The decision of the Full Bench in Nithyanandam
case was also as regards the authority of the Special Officer to admi
new members. In the circumstances, the decisions have to be seen an
understood in the context of the issue before the Courts and th
provisions as they existed at the time of consideration of th
question. The decision, in the circumstances, do not, in any manner
advance the cause of the petitioners.
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

118. Mr.R.Shanmugasundaram, learned senior counsel appearing fo
the petitioners in W.P.Nos.31749 of 2012, 1126 and 1127 of 2013
expressed his concern about the deemed admission of members, which
according to him, is a back-door entry, which may bring a change in th
composition of the society and hence, the amendment is bad. Further
under the back-door entry, even an ineligible member may get in an
there is no mechanism to check this.
119. The provision as regards the deemed admission appears in th
second proviso to Section 21(2), which reads as under:
" Section 21(2)(i):
(2) (i) In the case of every registered society, every
individual eligible for admission as a member of any such
society under the provisions of this Act, the Rules and
the bye-laws of the society shall, on application made in
such form and in such manner as maybe prescribed, be
admitted by the board or by the general body where there
is no board as a member of the society with effect from
the date of receipt of such application in the office of
such society:
Provided that the board or the general body, as the case
may be, may, for good and sufficient reasons to be
recorded in the minutes of the meeting at which the
application for admission is considered, refuse admission
to any individual and the decision of the board or the
general body, as the case may be, shall be communicated
to the individual:
Provided further that if the decision of the board or the
general body, as the case may be, on the application is
not communicated to the individual within a period of
sixty days from the date of receipt of the application in
the office of the society, the individual shall be deemed
to have been admitted as a member of such society, on the
sixtieth day after the date of receipt of the application
in the office of the society."
120. Admittedly, even under the unamended Section, the firs
proviso dealt with the deemed admission of members in the event of th
Board not passing any order on the application, that, on the expiry o
60 days' period from the date of receipt of the application in th
office of the society, such applicant would be deemed to have bee
admitted by the Board as a member of the Society. Admittedly, th
petitioners never had any grievance or questioned the legality of th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

above-said deemed membership as a back-door entry as bringing a chang
or altering the composition of the Society. Thus, after the amendmen
to Section 21(2) of the Act, in consonance with the substantive part o
Section 21(2)(i), the proviso to Section 21(2) also stood amended b
insertion of the words "general body in the absence of the board"
This, in effect, is only a continuation of what is inserted under th
substantive part of Section 21(2)(i) of the Act. Thus, all that th
present amendment does, is that the benefit that is there on deeme
admission will continue as before, that in the event of the board o
the general body in the absence of the board not taking any decision o
the application within 60 days of receipt of the application, suc
applicant will gain deemed membership in the society. In th
circumstances, applying the same reasoning as we have done in th
preceding paragraphs, we do not find any justifiable ground to accep
the submission made by the learned senior counsel.
121. It may be seen from the provisions of the Act that Section 2
(2)(ii) vests the authority on the Registrar to remove any individua
admitted under the deeming provision contained in the second proviso
either suo motu or on application at any time. The said provisio
under Section 21(2)(ii) reads as under:
" Section 21(2)(ii):
Notwithstanding anything contained in class (i), or
in any other provision of this Act, the Registrar
may, either suo motu or on application at any time,
by order and after recording the reasons in
writing, remove any individual deemed to have been
admitted as a member of the society under clause
(i) from such membership if such individual is not
eligible to be a member of such society under the
provisions of this Act, the Rules and the bye-laws
of the society:
Provided that an order under this clause shall
be passed within such period as may be prescribed.
"
122. The admission of a disqualified member is also subject t
sub-section (4) of Section 23, which provides that the Registrar shal
decide any question relating to such disqualification in respect o
admission of a member to a registered society.
123. Thus, contrary to the assertion of the petitioners, we fin
that the authority of the Board to admit new members traces its origi
only to Section 21(2) and not to the byelaws. In prescribing a
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

alternative authority in the event of there being no governing body
there exists no necessity to amend Section 32 of the Act. In th
circumstances, we reject the arguments of Mr.V.Raghavachari, learne
counsel appearing for the writ petitioners in W.P.Nos.24817 to 24820
23753 and 23754 of 2012.
124. Learned counsel appearing for the writ petitioners submitte
that if the general body is to have the authority to admit members
this would only promote unmerited persons' entry and this would lead t
arbitrary exercise of power and would become an area of abuse by th
general body.
125. Instead of holding the election, the State is adopting a
unfair means to admit new members through the general body. Hence
according to Mr.P.Wilson, learned senior counsel, the amendmen
manifests legislative arbitrariness. There is no nexus to the objec
sought to be achieved and the provision is detrimental to the ver
interests of the cooperative movement. He further pointed out that th
Act differentiates between societies with the Board and societie
without the Board. Such classification has no nexus with th
cooperative movement. In this connection, he placed reliance on th
decision reported in 2011 (6) MLJ 43 (State of Tamil Nadu V. K.Shya
Sunder). Further, the legislation is a colourable one and seeks to ge
over the decision of the Full Bench.
126. Mr.R.Shanmugasundaram, learned senior counsel, submitted tha
the amendment is primarily against the interests of the society. Wha
is in the interest of the society is primarily for the society t
decide. Thus, the admission of members being purely a matter of th
Society's choice, the State cannot direct the general body to admit ne
members. He referred to the decision reported in (2000) 9 SCC 29
(State of Maharashtra and others Vs. Karvenagar Sahakari Griha Rachan
Sanstha Maryadir and others), where the Apex Court pointed out that th
power of the State to issue directions in public interest cannot b
exercised so as to be prejudicial to the interests of the society.
127. As far as the contention of the petitioners as to th
possible abuse of power is concerned, as already noted, as per Sectio
21(2)(ii), admission of ineligible members can always be checked by th
Registrar, either suo motu or on application. The apprehension as t
the possible abuse of the power, however, per se, does not give rise t
any infringement of a constitutional provision. An Act cannot b
declared as unconstitutional solely on the ground of possibility of a
arbitrary exercise of power - Refer 1975 (1) SCR 1 (Maganla
Chhagganlal (P) Ltd. V. Municipal Corporation of Greater Bombay & Ors
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

- followed in (2011) 8 SCC 161 (Indian Council for Enviro-Legal Actio
V. Union of India).
128. As regards Mr.Wilson's submission on the differentia
treatment to societies with Board functioning and societies without th
Board having no nexus to the object of cooperative movement, the sai
argument has to be straight away rejected. The Tamil Nadu Cooperativ
Societies Act is a regulatory Act to provide for organisatio
management and supervision of cooperative societies in the State i
accordance with cooperative principles, such as open membership an
democratic management. In so providing for the organisation an
management in accordance with the democratic principles, the amendin
Act remedies the situation on the admission of new members brough
about by the absence of the board on whom the Act originally vested
With no election conducted to form the governing body for 11 years an
in the wake of the limited jurisdiction of the Special Officer t
manage the day-to-day affairs of the society, the State thought it fi
to address itself to the situation brought about by the absence of
governing body leading to non-admission of eligible members. Given th
object of forming a cooperative society with a laudable objective o
inculcating a spirit to work in a group freely on democratic principle
for rendering benefit to its members, the growth of the movement o
democratic principles itself depends on admission of new members int
the society. Article 19(1)(c) confers right on all citizens to form a
association. The right conferred on the citizens includes the right t
be associated with members of their choice whom they voluntarily admi
in the Association. If such a right to admit new members with whom th
members decide to associate is suspended by reason of the absence of
body empowered to admit, certainly, such situation itself is offensiv
of Article 19(1)(c) and it calls for remedial action by the State. I
the byelaws have provided for such a situation, then, by reason o
absence of such provision in the Act, perhaps, there may be no need fo
addressing such a situation through an amendment. The byelaws of th
society are regulated by the Act and the Rules and the same are to b
in accordance with the requirements of the Act and the Rules. Th
byelaws provided by the societies principally cover the areas assigne
to it, on topics which are left to it by the statute. In matter
relating to admission of members, the byelaws provide for th
eligibility criteria, apart from what is prescribed statutorily
Therefore, when the State amends the Section to provide for the gap i
the provisions arising on account of the absence of the Board throug
an amendment to the Section, the said amendment does not amount t
amending the byelaw or offending Article 19(1)(c) of the Constitutio
of India. On the other hand, it is the recognition of the ultimat
authority and the rights of the general body to admit persons t
associate with the society for the general betterment and achieving o
the objects of the society.
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

129. Thus, in remedying a situation which is transitory, there ca
be no classification of societies to result in an arbitrariness or t
have a nexus with the object of the cooperative movement. As of today
there are no two classes of societies as one with the Board and th
other without a Board, even to speak of discrimination. Hence, th
societies do not suffer any differential treatment. Even otherwise
when the amendment merely provides for a situation arising on accoun
of the absence of the Board, there is no such thing as two classes o
Societies to result in any discrimination. The question of raisin
such an issue itself does not arise herein. Apart from this, no tw
society similarly placed has come before this Court complaining of an
differential treatment even among these societies. In fact, th
amended provision only promotes and aids the cooperative movement
which, hitherto, has been under a spell of hybernation on account o
the society functioning just with a Special Officer and the existin
members with no scope for further growth.
130. We also reject the argument of the learned senior counse
that the provision is a colourable one, in so far as it seeks to ge
over the decision of the Full Bench reported in 2006 (1) CTC
(K.Nithyanandam Vs. State of Tamil Nadu). As already pointed out, th
Full Bench decision is concerned about the role of the Special Office
and his authority to admit new members. Section 21(2), as it the
stood, empowered the Board alone to admit new members. The statement o
objects and reasons clearly points out the defect that the amendmen
seeks to rectify and the amendment has not, in any manner, attempted t
get over the judgment of this Court. Thus, in providing for
situation caused by the suspension or absence of the Board to admit ne
members, the State rightly made the amendment making provision fo
admission through the general body.
131. In the decision reported in (2011) 9 SCC 286 (A.P.Dair
Development Corpn. Federation Vs. B.Narasimha Reddy), the Apex Cour
pointed out that the action of the legislature to be held as violativ
of Article 14, should ordinarily be manifestly arbitrary. It held:
" It is a settled legal proposition that Article 14
of the Constitution strikes at arbitrariness
because an action that is arbitrary, must
necessarily involve negation of equality. This
doctrine of arbitrariness is not restricted only to
executive actions, but also applies to legislature.
Thus, a party has to satisfy that the action was
reasonable, not done in unreasonable manner or
capriciously or at pleasure without adequate
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

determining principle, rational, and has been done
according to reason or judgment, and certainly does
not depend on the will alone. However, the action
of legislature, violative of Article 14 of the
Constitution, should ordinarily be manifestly
arbitrary. There must be a case of substantive
unreasonableness in the statute itself for
declaring the Act ultra vires of Article 14 of the
Constitution. (Vide: Ajay Hasia etc. v. Khalid
Mujib Sehravardi and Ors. etc. AIR 1981 SC 487;
Reliance Airport Developers (P) Ltd. v. Airports
Authority of India and Ors. MANU/SC/4912/2006 :
(2006) 10 SCC 1; Bidhannagar (Salt Lake) Welfare
Assn. v. Central Valuation Board and Ors.
MANU/SC/2553/2007 : AIR 2007 SC 2276; Grand
Kakatiya Sheraton Hotel and Towers Employees and
Workers Union v. Srinivasa Resorts Limited and
Ors.MANU/SC/0436/2009 : AIR 2009 SC 2337; and State
of Tamil Nadu and Ors. v. K. Shyam Sunder and Ors.
MANU/SC/0911/2011 : 2011 8 SCALE 474. "
Thus, applying the law declared, we have no hesitation in rejecting th
submissions made based on Article 14 of the Constitution of India.
132. As far as the submission made by Mr.R.Shanmugasundaram
learned senior counsel, placing reliance on the decision reported i
(2000) 9 SCC 295 (State of Maharashtra and others Vs. Karvenaga
Sahakari Griha Rachana Sanstha Maryadir and others) is concerned, we d
not find that the said decision would be of any assistance to th
petitioners.
133. There is no dispute on the proposition of law that admissio
of new members is a subject entirely within the discretion of th
society. In the decision reported in (2000) 9 SCC 295 (State o
Maharashtra and others Vs. Karvenagar Sahakari Griha Rachana Sansth
Maryadir and others), the facts were that the State of Maharashtr
directed the cooperative housing societies to amend their byelaws so a
to enable the plot holders to construct multi-storeyed building wit
more than one residential tenement on their plots and to form a societ
of flat owners of the multi-storeyed buildings, which shall be a membe
of the housing society and represented by its representative in th
housing society. The cooperative societies challenged the circular tha
they completely destroyed the basis of such societies and would promot
commercialisation of housing societies meant for individuals on th
basis of the tenant-ownership and hence, violative of Article 19(1)(c
of the Constitution of India. In this context, the Apex court pointe
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

out to Section 14, which enabled the Registrar to call upon any societ
to amend its byelaws if the same appeared to him that the amendment i
necessary. It observed in paragraph 6 of the judgment, as follows:
"6. From the above discussion, it is clear that
though the power is conferred on the Registrar to
direct amendment of the bye-laws of the Society,
yet the paramount consideration is the interest of
the Society. So also the power of the State
Government to issue directions in public interest
cannot be exercised so as to be prejudicial to the
interest of the Society. In our view, what is in
the interest of the society is primarily for the
society alone to decide and it is not for an
outside agency to say. Where, however, the
Government or the Registrar exercises statutory
power of issuing directions to amend the bye-laws,
such directions should satisfy the requirement of
the interest of the Society. In the instant case,
having regard to the nature of the Society and its
objectives, referred to above, and having also
regard to the fact that the Society in the case of
5th respondent has turned down his request for the
grant of permission by overwhelming majority, we
are unable to say that the amendment directed by
Government is in the interest of the Society. The
High Court is, therefore, right in quashing the
impugned directions/circulars. "
134. The judgment is distinguishable and has no application to th
issues raised. As already seen, the amended provision merely provide
for a situation warranted by the absence of the Board. In admittin
new members, the general body has to necessarily go by the provision
of the Act, Rules and the byelaws, prescribing the eligibility. Thus
the amended Section nowhere directs the society to admit members who d
not satisfy the eligibility criteria. The amended Section does no
interfere with the discretion of the society so as to compel th
Society on the admission of every inidividual applicant by the mer
score of his satisfying the eligibility prescribed under the Act an
the bye-laws for admission as a member. Hence, the stand taken by th
learned counsel by placing reliance on the decision reported in (2000
9 SCC 295 (State of Maharashtra and others Vs. Karvenagar Sahakar
Griha Rachana Sanstha Maryadir and others), fails.
135. We also reject the arguments of Mr.V.Raghavachari, learne
counsel appearing for the petitioners in W.P.Nos.24817 to 24820, 2375
and 23754 of 2012, based on the judgments reported in AIR 1961 Cal 25
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

(Murarka Paint and Varnish Works Ltd. Vs. Mohanlal Murarka and Ors
and 1968 KLJ 19 (The Suburban Bank Private Ltd. Vs. Thariath an
others).
136. As already seen, a reading of the amended provision show
that it has not, in any manner, interfered with the free will of th
society in admitting new persons. Thus, there being no such restrictio
spoken to in the amendment or discernible from the amendment, as to th
authority of the general body to admit new members, we do not find an
justifiable ground to uphold the contentions of the petitioners
Consequently, the writ petitions are liable to be dismissed. We hol
that the amended provision does not violate Articles 14 or 19(1)(c), o
for that matter, Part-IXB of the Constitution of India and hence, ar
perfectly valid.
137. Mr.P.Wilson, learned senior counsel, submitted that th
amendment permitting admission of new members by the general body i
against the interests of the society, that such admission would onl
make the existing members a minority; that the amendment has bee
introduced by the State only with an ulterior motive to fill th
societies with members of their choice.
138. We reject the contention that the admission of new member
would make the existing members a minority. If admission of ne
members by the Board is a welcome exercise to the petitioners and henc
would not make the existing members a minority, we fail to see an
logic in the contention of the petitioners that the admission by th
general body would make the existing members, a minority. Th
situation spoken to in the decision reported in AIR 1971 SC 96
(Damyanthi V. Union of India) as to the admission of new members makin
the existing members a minority is a totally different one, for, in th
said decision, it was clearly noted that under the impugned enactment
the existing members had no say in the matter of admitting new member
which was totally done by the Government. In the decision reported i
(2011) 9 SCC 286 @ 308, (A.P.Dairy Development Corpn. Federation Vs
B.Narasimha Reddy), the Apex Court observed as follows:
" The constitutional right to freely associate with
others encompasses associational ties designed to
further the social, legal and economic benefits of
the members of the association. By statutory
interventions, the State is not permitted to change
the fundamental character of the association or
alter the composition of the society itself. The
significant encroachment upon associational freedom
cannot be justified on the basis of any interest of
the Government. However, when the association gets
registered under the Co-operative Societies Act, it
is governed by the provisions of the Act and rules
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

framed thereunder. "
139. Pointing out that the duty of the State as a continuing bod
to continue and carry on an unfinished job of the previous Government
for the reason "the action is that of the State" within the meaning o
Article 12 of the Constitution of India, which continues to subsist
the Supreme Court observed that in this, the motive of the legislatur
is inconsequential. In the decision reported in (2011) 6 MLJ 43 (Stat
of Tamil Nadu Vs. K.Shyam Sundar), the Supreme Court observed a
follows:
" 22. It has consistently been held by this Court that
the doctrine of malafide does not involve any
question of bonafide or malafide on the part of
legislature as in such a case, the Court is
concerned to a limited issue of competence of the
particular legislature to enact a particular law.
If the legislature is competent to pass a
particular enactment, the motives which impelled it
to an act are really irrelevant. On the other hand,
if the legislature lacks competence, the question
of motive does not arrive at all. Therefore,
whether a statute is constitutional or not is,
thus, always a question of power of the legislature
to enact that Statute.
Motive of the legislature while enacting a Statute
is inconsequential: "Malice or motive is beside the
point, and it is not permissible to suggest
parliamentary incompetence on the score of mala
fides."
The legislature, as a body, cannot be accused of
having passed a law for an extraneous purpose. This
kind of "transferred malice" is unknown in the
field of legislation.
[See: K.C. Gajapati Narayan Deo and Ors. v. State
of Orissa MANU/SC/0014/1953 : AIR 1953 SC 375; R.S.
Joshi, Sales Tax Officer, Gujarat and Ors. v. Ajit
Mills Limited and Anr. MANU/SC/0300/1977 : AIR 1977
SC 2279; K. Nagaraj and Ors. v. State of Andhra
Pradesh and Anr. MANU/SC/0343/1985 : AIR 1985 SC
551; Welfare Assocn. A.R.P., Maharashtra and Anr.
v. Ranjit P. Gohil and Ors. MANU/SC/0129/2003 : AIR
2003 SC 1266; and State of Kerala and Anr. v.
Peoples Union for Civil Liberties, Kerala State
Unit and Ors. MANU/SC/1302/2009 : (2009) 8 SCC 46].
III. LAWS CONTRAVENING ARTICLE 13(2):
23. The legislative competence can be adjudged with
reference to Articles 245 and 246 of the
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Constitution read with the three lists given in the
Seventh Schedule as well as with reference to
Article 13(2) of the Constitution which prohibits
the State from making any law which takes away or
abridges the rights conferred by Part-III of the
Constitution and provides that any law made in
contravention of this Clause shall, to the extent
of contravention be void. "
140. Thus a challenge to the constitutionality of a provision o
statute could be made only on the ground of lack of legislativ
competence, violation of Part III of the Constitution of India an
which includes reasonableness of the law made. Referring to D.D.Bas
on the Shorter Constitution of India 14th Edition 2009, in the decisio
reported in JT 2012 9 SC 166 (Namit Sharma Vs. Union of India (UOI),
decision relied on by the learned Additional Advocate General, th
Supreme Court observed:
"10. An enacted law may be constitutional or
unconstitutional. Traditionally, this Court had
provided very limited grounds on which an enacted
law could be declared unconstitutional. They were
legislative competence, violation of Part III of
the Constitution and reasonableness of the law. The
first two were definite in their scope and
application while the cases falling in the third
category remained in a state of uncertainty. With
the passage of time, the law developed and the
grounds for unconstitutionality also widened. D.D.
Basu in the 'Shorter Constitution of India'
(Fourteenth Edition, 2009) has detailed, with
reference to various judgments of this Court, the
grounds on which the law could be invalidated or
could not be invalidated. Reference to them can be
made as follows:
Grounds of unconstitutionality. - A law may be
unconstitutional on a number of grounds:
i. Contravention of any fundamental right,
specified in Part III of the Constitution. (Ref.
Under Article 143, (Ref. MANU/SC/0048/1964 : AIR
1965 SC 745 (145): 1965(1) SCR 413)
ii. Legislating on a subject which is not assigned
to the relevant legislature by the distribution of
powers made by the 7th Sch., read with the
connected Articles. (Ref. Under Article 143
MANU/SC/0048/1964 : AIR 1965 SC 745)
iii. Contravention of any of the mandatory
provisions of the Constitution which impose
limitations upon the powers of a Legislature, e.g.,
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Article 301. (Ref. Atiabari Tea Co. v. State of
Assam MANU/SC/0030/1960 : AIR 1961 SC 232)
iv. In the case of a State law, it will be invalid
in so far as it seeks to operate beyond the
boundaries of the State. (State of Bombay v.
Chamarbaughwala R.M.D. MANU/SC/0019/ 1957 : AIR
1957 SC 699)
v. That the Legislature concerned has abdicated its
essential legislative function as assigned to it by
the Constitution or has made an excessive
delegation of that power to some other body.
Hamdard Dawakhana Wakf v. Union of India
MANU/SC/0016/1959 : AIR 1960 SC 554 (568) "
141. In paragraph 13, it further observed as follows:
 "13. A law which violates the fundamental right of
a person is void. In such cases of violation, the
Court has to examine as to what factors the Court
should weigh while determining the
constitutionality of a statute. First and the
foremost, as already noticed, is the competence of
the legislature to make the law. The wisdom or
motive of the legislature in making it is not a
relative consideration. The Court should examine
the provisions of the statute in light of the
provisions of the Constitution (e.g. Part III),
regardless of how it is actually administered or is
capable of being administered. In this regard, the
Court may consider the following factors as noticed
in D.D. Basu (supra).
(a) The possibility of abuse of a statute does not
impart to it any element of invalidity.
(b) Conversely, a statute which violates the
Constitution cannot be pronounced valid merely
because it is being administered in a manner which
might not conflict with the constitutional
requirements.
In the case of Charan Lal Sahu v. UOI (1990) 1 SCC
614 (667) (para 13), MUKHERJEE, C.J. made an
unguarded statement, viz., that
In judging the Constitutional validity of the Act,
the subsequent events, namely, how the Act has
worked out, have to be looked into.
It can be supported only on the test of 'direct and
inevitable effect' and, therefore, needs to be
explained in some subsequent decision.
(c) When the constitutionality of a law is
challenged on the ground that it infringes a
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

fundamental right, what the Court has to consider
is the 'direct and inevitable effect' of such law.
(d) There is presumption in favour of
constitutionality of statutes. The law courts can
declare the legislative enactment to be an invalid
piece of legislation only in the even of gross
violation of constitutional sanctions. "
142. Thus, guided by the law declared, we reject the contention o
the petitioners based on lack of bona fides in bringing out th
amendment as well as in the reasonableness of the provision. Thus, w
have no hesitation in holding that the amended provision of Section 2
(2) is constitutionally valid and does not offend Articles 14, 19(1)(c
and Part IXB of the Constitution of India.
143. Mr.P.Wilson, learned senior counsel, referred to the decisio
reported in (2009) 5 SCC 342 (Grand Kakatiya Sheraton Hotel and Tower
Employees and Workers Union v. Srinivasa Resorts Limited and Ors.) tha
even if the law cannot be declared ultra vires on the ground o
unreasonableness, applying Wednesbury's principle, the amending Act ha
to be struck down. He relied on the observation in paragraph 27, whic
reads as under:
"27. The High Court took into consideration the
provisions of the Gratuity Act and found that the
minimum period of service therein was five years,
as also for the government servants of the State,
the minimum qualifying period for earning gratuity
was 10 years of service. comparatively, the High
Court came to the conclusion that limiting this
period of long, continuous and satisfactory service
only to one year was unreasonable and
discriminatory. The High Court also severely
commented on the provisions that in case of death
or disablement, the condition of completionof one
year of service was also not necessary. "
144. We have already seen in the preceding paragraphs that th
amending Act does not, in any manner, interfere with the discretion o
the society to admit new members. Thus, with the right of the societ
to admit new members in fact preserved, we do not find an
unreasonableness in the provision to strike it down. The observation o
the Apex Court, does not, in any manner, help the petitioners in thi
case.
145. This takes us to the issue raised on the validity of th
amended rules.
146. Section 21(2) states that a person submitting an applicatio
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

for admission as a member must submit the application in such form an
in such manner as may be prescribed under the Rules.
147. Rule 27 of the Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies Rules, 1988
provides for the form of application and the manner of admission as
member. It states:
27. Form of application for, and manner of,
admission as a member.--
(1) Every individual seeking admission as a member
of any society under clause (i) of sub-section (2)
of Section 21 shall make an application in Form
No.16.
(2) The application shall be sent to the society by
registered post with acknowledgement due or
personally handed over under acknowledgement to an
officer or servant of the society authorised in
this behalf by the society.
(3) Every individual seeking admission as a member
of any society under clause (i) of sub-section (2)
of Section 21 shall, along with the application for
admission pay the value of at least one share of
the first instalment of the share, where share
capital is payable in instalments and also the
entrance fees. Such payment may be made either by
sending the required amount by money order or
postal order sent by registered post with
acknowledgement due or by making direct remittance
into the society or by any other mode approved for
the purpose by the society or the Registrar.
(4) The Secretary or where there is no Secretary,
the Chief Executive or where there is no Secretary
or Chief Executive, the President shall place or
cause to be placed all applications for membership
before the board for its decision and arrange to
communicate the decision of the board granting or
refusing the grant admission to the applicant
within a period of sixty days from the date of
receipt of the application in the office of the
society.
(5) Every member admitted or deemed to have been
admitted as such, shall, for exercising the rights
of a member, pay, within a period of one month from
the date of admission or the date on which he is
deemed to have been admitted as a member, all the
amounts required of him under the bye-laws. "
148. In the face of the authority specified in the Rule to receiv
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

and place the application before the general body, we do not find an
ground to accept the plea of the petitioner that there is no competen
authority to receive the application and scrutinize the same fo
placing it before the general body.
149. It may be seen that consequent on the amendment to Section 2
(2), necessary amendments are also brought in, in Rule 27(1). Th
amended Rule reads as follows:
"In the said Rules,—
(1) in rule 27, for sub-rule (4), the following sub-rule
shall be substituted, namely:-
“(4) The Secretary or where there is no secretary, the
chief executive or where there is no secretary or chief
executive, the president shall place or cause to be
placed all applications for membership before the board
or the general body, where there is no board for its
decision and arrange to communicate the decision of the
board or the general body, as the case may be, granting
or refusing to grant admission to the applicant within a
period of sixty days from the date of receipt of the
application in the office of the society”. "
150. The grievance of the petitioners is that even before the Rul
and the Forms prescribed thereunder were amended, applications fo
admission as member in the society in the unamended form have been mad
and submitted before the Secretary/Special Officer and new members ar
admitted by the general body. Further, there was no notice given t
the members in accordance with Rule 45 on the convening of the genera
body to admit new members.
151. Form 16 to be filed by the applicant seeking membership
prescribed as per Rule 27, is as follows:

I request you to admit me as a Member of your Society. I furnis

and Name of the Post Office in which

14 Whether nomination in Form No.17 is
enclosed:
I declare that I am qualified to become a Member of th

(Tamil Nadu Act 30 of 1983), the Tamil Nadu Co-operative Societie

to the best of my knowledge and belief.

for membership of the Society contemplated in the Tamil Nadu Co
operative Societies Act, 1983 (Tamil Nadu Act 30 of 1983), the Tami
Nadu Co-operative Societies Rules, 1988 or the Bylaws of the Society.
