152. The amendment to Section 21(2) was originally brought throug
Ordinance No.14/2012 on 03.08.2012. The said Ordinance No.14/2012 o
the Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies (Third Amendment) Ordinance, 2012
promulgated on 3
rd August 2012 by the Government, was gazetted o
03.08.2012, coming into effect from 03.08.2012. The Ordinance becam
an Act under Article 37 of 2012, gazetted on 16.11.2012 and receive
the assent on 14
th November 2012. The Rules were amended on 19.11.201
vide G.O.(Ms).No.142, Cooperative, Food and Consumer Protection (CJ1
Department dated 19.11.2012. The said Rule was gazetted on 05.12.2012
153. Learned Additional Advocate General submitted that so long a
the Rules are in tune with the intention of the Section and even if th
same are not amended, the same will apply in so far as they are not i
conflict with the Section. The absence of any modification to the for
at the time when the applications are made, however, will not make the
bad in law to be rejected.
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

154. It may be noted that membership in a society is a matter o
contract and the society itself is the result of an agreement betwee
the members to form an association with a common object. As see
already, a reading of Section 21(2) shows that a person seekin
admission has to make an application in such form and in such manner a
may be prescribed and Rule 25 enjoins on an applicant to give th
declaration that he possesses all qualifications prescribed fo
membership of the society under the Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societie
Act, 1983, Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies Rules, 1988 and the byelaw
of the Society. Thus, the application assumes significance in th
context of the eligibility of a member. Although every eligibl
member cannot, as a matter of right, claim membership in a society o
the ground that he satisfies the eligibility criteria, yet, a rejectio
of the claim at the threshold fundamentally stems from th
ineligibility of the applicant not satisfying the eligibilit
qualification prescribed therefor. Thus the importance of the For
assumes significance in the context of the declaration from th
eligible member that he is qualified to apply for membership and tha
he has not suffered any disqualification for membership. Given th
object of the society as indicated in the byelaws with which a
applicant seeks to associate and work for the fulfilment of tha
object, the question as to whether the delayed amendment to the Rule
or the absence of amendment in accordance with the amended Section t
insert the general body in the Form would vitiate the amendin
provision, arises for consideration by this Court.
155. In the decision reported in 1987 SCR (1)1054: (1987) 1 SC
658 (B.K.Srinivasan & Others Vs. State of Karnataka & Others), th
Supreme Court reiterated the principle that a subordinate legislation
to take effect, must be published and promulgated in some suitabl
manner and it takes effect from the date of such publication o
promulgation.
156. Courts have held that being subordinate legislation, the
must be consistent with the substantive provisions of the Act an
cannot have the effect of either enlarging or restricting or curtailin
the content and the amplitude of the provision. - Refer (1996) 2 SC
405 (Delhi Science Forum Vs. Union of India) and (2004) 8 SCC 74
(Mahachandra Prasad Singh (Dr.) Vs. Chairman, Bihar Legislativ
Council). Rules are to be in consonance with the substantive provisio
and must supplement the Act. Thus, when the amending Act confer
authority on the general body to admit new members in the absence o
the Board, the authority of the general body is not taken away merel
by reason of the Rule or Form not being amended accordingly when th
notice for admission by the general body was issued. The Rule and th
Form made thereunder prescribe the particulars to be submitted by a
intending member. It thus prescribes the contents of the applicatio
and has nothing to do with naming the authority who shall consider th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

application. Thus the Rule cannot be elevated to the status of
substantive law to hold that the society had no authority to admit ne
members through general body till the Form is amended or by reason o
an applicant using the old Form.
157. The decisions relied on by the learned Additional Advocat
General reported in AIR 1962 SC 113 (Bhikraj Jaipuria Vs. Union o
India), 1980 Supp. SCC 22 (Talaksi Malsi Sawala Vs. State o
Maharashtra) and (2011) 2 SCC 1 (CIT Vs. Tulsyan NEC Ltd.), point ou
that even if the Rules are not amended in consonance with th
provisions of the statute, Rules have to be read only mutatis mutandis
the provisions. Thus the Form prescribed being in the nature of
declaration by an applicant containing the details on eligibility, th
subsequent insertion in the Act, of the words "general body in th
absence of a board" and on the Form cannot, in any manner, make th
proceedings taken on the basis of the old Form, illegal. In th
circumstances, we reject the contention of the petitioners.
158. As regards the service of notice for convening the genera
body meeting, Rule 46(2) prescribes the service of notice. The sai
Rule reads as under:
" 46. General meeting of societies.--
(1) ...
(2) The notice of the general meeting, whether
ordinary or special, shall be sent by the society
to the members by one or more of the following
modes, namely:-
(a) by giving or tendering it to the member under
his acknowledgement; or
(b) if the member is not found by giving or
tendering it to some adult member of his family
under his acknowledgement; or
(c) by post under certificate of posting; or
(d) by publication in one of more local newspapers
having circulation in the area of operation of the
society and approved by the Government for issue of
Government advertisement.
The society shall, in addition to sending
notice by one of the modes aforesaid, also display
the notice in the board at the office of the
society or in one or more public places within the
area of operation of the society. "
159. As is evident from the reading of the sub rule, i
contemplates service of notice in either one or more of the method
specified therein. Apart from the mode specified, the society has t
display the notice in the board at the office of the society or in on
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

or more public places within the area of operation of the society.
160. Having regard to the clear provision on the service o
notice, compliance of any one or more of the modes thus bein
sufficient, learned Additional Advocate General submitted that th
notice through publication being one of the contemplated methods, ther
is full compliance of the provisions of the Rules. The use of the wor
"or" has to be read as disjunctive and the observance of either one o
more need not be sequential. In the decision reported in (1980) 1 SC
158 (Municipal Corporation of Delhi Vs. Tek Chand Bhatia), the Ape
Court referred to Stroud's Judicial Dictionary 3
rd edition, Vol.I a
page 135, which reads as under:
" In Stroud's Judicial Dictionary, 3rd Edn., vol. 1,
it is stated st p. 135: "And" has generally a
cumulative sense, requiring the fulfillment of all
the conditions that it joins together, and herein
it is the antithesis of OR. Sometimes, however,
even in such a connection, it is, by force of a
context, read as "or"."
161. In the background of the law enunciated, in the absence o
anything indicated in the Rule as to the compliance of more than on
mode of service as absolute requirement and considering the word
employed "notice of the general meeting whether ordinary or specia
shall be sent by the society to member by one or more of the followin
modes, namely", the intention is clear that the legislature intended t
read "or" as disjunctive only and that there is no need to follo
either one or more in the order stated in the Rule. The petitioner
herein do not deny that in all the cases, notice of the meeting wa
published in the local daily. Thus going by the clear language of th
Rule, we do not find any justification to read "or", a disjunctive, a
a conjunction. Consequently, the submission of Mr.V.Ravi, learne
counsel appearing for the petitioners in W.P.Nos.25993, 27055 to 2705
26440, 26441, 27746, 27792, 28530, 28800, 30305 and 33951 of 2012
stands rejected.
162. Mr.V.Ravi, learned counsel appearing for the petitioners i
W.P.No.25993, 27055 to 27059, 26440, 26441, 27746, 27792, 28530, 28800
30305 and 33951 of 2012, submitted that the present amendment has no
got the assent of the President under Article 255 or Article 254(2) o
the Constitution of India. The Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies Act
1983, when enacted, received the assent of the President of India
Hence, any amendment to the provision must necessarily go through th
same procedure under Article 255 of the Constitution of India.
163. Learned Advocate General, however, countered this b
contending that being a State subject falling under Entry 32, List I
of the Seventh Schedule to the Constitution of India, the State ha
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

every authority to bring an amendment and this does not require th
assent of the President. Being regulatory in nature, the sai
amendment is not in conflict with any of the provisions of the Centra
enactment or a subject falling in the Concurrent List. Hence, th
objection of the petitioners cannot be sustained.
164. We agree with the submissions of the State. Article 255 o
the Constitution of India, reads as follows:
" 255. Requirements as to recommendations and previous
sanctions to be regarded as matters of procedure only.--
No Act of Parliament or of the Legislature of a State [The
words and letters "specified in Part A or Part B of the
First Schedule " omitted by the Constitution (Seventh
Amendment) Act, 1956, S.29 and Sch.], and no provision in
any such Act, shall be invalid by reason only that some
recommendation or previous sanction required by this
Constitution was not given, if assent to that Act was
given --
(a) where the recommendation required was that of the
Governor, either by the Governor or by the President;
(b) where the recommendation required was that of the
Rajpramukh, either by the Rajpramkh or by the President;
(c) Whether the recommendation or previous sanction
required was that of the President, by the President. "
165. As far as the requirement of the assent of the President to
State legislation is concerned, such contingency would normally aris
only under the stated circumstances mentioned in the Act, say, as i
the case of a State legislation falling for consideration under Articl
304. As far as the Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies Act is concerned
learned Advocate General pointed out that at the time of enactment, b
reason of Section 175 referring to the non-applicability of certai
laws – viz., Companies Act, Section 90 - Arbitration and Conciliatio
Act, 1996, exclusion of the provision under Section 34 of the Civi
Procedure Code to the decisions passed or award made under Section 9
(8) of the Cooperative Societies Act, the original Act was assented t
by the president . But the situation now is not the same. When th
State is fully competent to legislate on matters relating t
cooperative societies under Entry 32 List II of Seventh Schedule to th
Constitution of India, the petitioners cannot contend that for want o
assent, the amendment fails.
166. We agree with the submissions of the learned Advocat
General. Admittedly, legislation on cooperative societies is a subjec
falling under Entry 32 List II of the Seventh Schedule to th
Constitution of India. There is nothing in the amended provision t
show that it falls for consideration under any of the state
circumstances under Article 254 or Article 255 of the Constitution o
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

India. Learned counsel Mr.Ravi referred to the decision reported i
(2012) 7 SCC 106 (State of Kerala and Ors. Vs. Mar Appraem Kuri Compan
Ltd. and Anr). The said decision has no relevance to the issue, sinc
the said decision was concerned about the validity of the Keral
Chitties Act, 1975, which had the requisite Presidential assent
became void and stood pro tanto repealed on account of the Centra
Legislation Chit Fund Act, 1982. In the circumstances, the Keral
Finance Act 7 of 2002 introducing Section 4(1)(a) in Kerala Chittie
Act, 1975, was also void, as the State Legislation stood denuded of th
authority to enact the 2002 Act - Public Premises (Eviction o
Unauthorised Occupants) Act, 1971, falling under Entry 6 and 7 of Lis
III of the Constitution. The Apex Court pointed out that Entry 7 Lis
III deals with the subject of "contract". It also covers specia
contracts. Chitties are special contracts. Thus, the Parliament an
the State Legislature are competent to enact a law with respect to suc
contracts. The Apex Court pointed out:
" 47. The question of repugnancy between
parliamentary legislation and State legislation
arises in two ways. First, where the legislations,
though enacted with respect to matters in their
allotted spheres, overlap and conflict. Second,
where the two legislations are with respect to
matters in the Concurrent List and there is a
conflict. In both the situations, the parliamentary
legislation will predominate, in the first, by
virtue of non obstante clause in Article 246(1); in
the second, by reason of Article 254(1).
50. Broadly speaking, law-making is exclusively the
function of the legislatures (see Articles 79 and
168). The President and the Governor are a part of
the Union or the legislatures of the States. As far
as Parliament is concerned, the legislative process
is complete as soon as the procedure prescribed by
Article 107 of the Constitution and connected
provisions are followed and the Bill passed by both
the Houses of Parliament has received the assent of
the President under Article 111. Similarly, a State
legislation becomes an Act as soon as a Bill has
been passed by the State Legislature and it has
received the assent of the Governor in accordance
with Article 200. It is only in the situation
contemplated by Article 254(2) that a State
legislation is required to be reserved for
consideration and assent by the President. Thus,
irrespective of the date of enforcement of a
parliamentary or State enactment, a Bill becomes an
Act and comes on the statute book immediately on
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

receiving the assent of the President or the
Governor, as the case may be, which assent has got
to be published in the Official Gazette. "
167. Thus the decision is concerned about cases falling under Lis
III, where the State and the Union have concurrent provisions wit
respect to subjects enacted in List III. It observed that th
intention to override the State law was clearly manifested in th
Central Act and Section 3 made it clear that the provisions of th
Central Act shall have effect, notwithstanding anything to the contrar
contained in any other law for the time being in force. Learne
counsel also referred to the decision reported in (2002) 8 SCC 18
(Kaiser-I-Hind Pvt. Ltd. and Ors. Vs. National Textile Corporatio
(Maharashtra North) Ltd. and Ors.). The said decision also does no
help the petitioner where the issue was as regards the repugnanc
between the Bombay Rents, Hotel and Lodging House Rates Control Act
1947 and Public Premises (Eviction of Unauthorised Occupants) Act
1971. A similar contention as is now raised was raised in the contex
of the amendment brought to the Tamil Nadu Tax on Entry of Moto
Vehicles Act. In the decision reported in 1997 (3) CTC 255 (R. Gandh
And Anr. vs Union Of India), this Court dealt with the question i
extenso and rejected the plea of the petitioner based on Articles 25
(2) and 255 of the Constitution of India. The law declared by thi
Court answers the question here too. In the circumstances, we rejec
the contention taken by Mr.Ravi, learned counsel appearing for th
petitioners in W.P.Nos.25993, 27055 to 27059, 26440, 26441, 2774
27792, 28530, 28800, 30305 and 33951 of 2012.
168. Learned Additional Advocate General produced before us, th
copies of the Cooperative Societies Act of the States of Goa, Bihar
Jharkhand and Mizoram and contended that similar provisions as i
Section 21(2) of the Tamil Nadu Act are available in thes
legislations. Mr.V.Raghavachari, learned counsel appearing for th
writ petitioners in W.P.Nos.24817 to 24820, 23753 and 23754 of 2012
submitted that the availability of provisions in other State enactment
cannot, in any manner, be pressed into service to support the amendmen
now under challenge. These Acts, with the provisions on the authorit
confined on the general assembly, have come into effect, even befor
the Amending Act to Tamil Nadu Cooperative Societies Act, 1983
Pointing out to the specific provisions of other States' enactmen
which specified the general body as having authority to admit ne
members whenever the Board is not functioning, he submitted that whe
specific power is entrusted to the Board by the bye laws, wit
qualitative difference existing between the Board and the general body
the power given to the Board cannot be amended without amendment to th
byelaws. Thus, the contract providing for management, cannot, in an
manner, be interfered with through the amendment. In this connection
he relied on the decision reported in AIR 1961 Cal 251 (Murarka Pain
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

and Varnish Works Ltd. Vs. Mohanlal Murarka and Ors.).
169. We reject the reasoning of the learned counsel appearing fo
the petitioners. The question raised herein is about the authority o
the State to bring an amendment to confer authority on the general bod
to admit new members. The test to the constitutionality of th
provision does not rest on the argument as to whether the Stat
contained originally such a provision or not.
170. We have already discussed in the preceding paragraphs tha
the provision on conferring authority on the Board under the byela
itself is traceable to Section 21(2) only and not independent of it, t
contend that such provision made in the byelaw cannot be touched by th
State through an amendment to Section 21(2), whereby, in the absence o
the Board alone, the general body will have the authority to admit ne
members. The consistent view of the Courts on the role of the genera
body is that it is the ultimate authority of a registered society.
 171. Even though Section 175 specifically excludes th
applicability of the Companies Act, Section 39 of the Act treats
registered cooperative society as a body corporate by the name unde
which it is registered.
"39. Societies to be Bodies Corporate.-- The registration
of a Society shall render it a Body Corporate by the name
under which it is registered, with perpetual succession
and a common seal and with power to hold property, to
enter into contracts, to institute and defend suits and
other legal proceedings and to do all things necessary for
the purposes for which it is constituted. "
172. Thus, on the admission of a member into a society, he lose
his individual identity and his individual interest yields to that o
the society's interest. Hence, the interest of the member is no les
or greater than that of the society. Thus, given a statutory corporat
status, the ultimate authority in the society as in the case of
company, vests with the general body, although subject to th
provisions of the Act, the Rules and the byelaws. Thus, when the Ac
conferred the authority on the Board originally to admit new members
it is not as though an authority which the general body does no
possess, was conferred on the Board. Recognising the fact that th
ultimate authority rested on the members consisting the society whic
is a body corporate, the Amending Act merely regulated a situatio
brought about by the absence of a Board.
173. In the decision reported in AIR 1953 Mad 520 (B.N.Viswanatha
And Anr. vs Tiffin'S Baryt Asbestos), arising under the Companies Act
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

1913, a Division Bench of this Court considered the situation brough
about by the absence of the Board. The brief facts are that a compan
was incorporated in 1945 and its first Directors were five in numbe
and there was a coopted member. With the resignation of four of th
directors, only two were there. After 30.12.1948, there was no annua
meeting held. In this background, one of the shareholders filed a
application before this Court under Section 79(3) of the Companies Act
1913, equivalent to Section 186 of the Companies Act, 1956, for
direction to convene a general body meeting by a Commissioner and fo
the appointment of an independent Chairman to preside over the meeting
174. As per the order of this Court, a meeting was held, wherei
the general body elected the Directors. This was challenged in a
application before this Court. On the dismissal of the applicatio
referring the applicants to seek suit remedy, a suit was also filed b
two shareholders for a declaration that the election of the Director
was void. The suit was dismissed. On the appeal, one of th
contentions taken was that the authority of the general body under th
Articles of the Company was only to appoint Directors in the place o
those who retire at the annual meeting. The power to appoint Director
was delegated to the Board of Directors under the Articles and hence
the election of six Directors beyond the competence of the general bod
meeting and hence was in contravention of the Articles of Association.
175. The said stand was countered by the respondent company tha
even if the power to appoint additional Directors was exclusivel
vested with the Board, yet, the resolution of the general body i
appointing the additional Directors should be upheld. Therefore, a
the time of the meeting, there were no Board of Directors, whic
validly functioned under the Articles and that the General Body had th
inherent power, which, it could then exercise to appoint Directors fo
enabling the company to function.
176. This Court held that at the time of the annual meeting, ther
were no Directors validly appointed in the Board and placing relianc
on the decision in the case of 'Isle of Weight Rly. Co. v. Tahordin
(1884) 25 Ch D 320 (D) and 'Barron v. Potter', (1914) 1 Ch 895 (E
this Court held that in the absence of the board or it suffering an
disability, the members had the right to elect the Directors at th
general body meeting.
177. Thus the principle one may deduce from this is, a company ha
inherent power to take all steps to ensure its proper working and thi
includes the power to appoint Directors. It can delegate this power t
the Board of Directors to appoint Directors and such decisions by th
Board are binding on the company. But where a Board is unable to ac
or where there is no legally constituted Board, the authority delegate
to the Board lapses and the members can exercise the right inherent i
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

them, to appoint Directors. Thus, this Court held that where there i
no board to elect the Directors, the members had the right to elect th
Directors at the annual meeting.
178. Extending the above principle to the case on hand
admittedly, the Board consists of members elected by the General Body
The Board is defined under Article 243ZH(b) to mean the Board o
Directors or the general body of a cooperative society, by whateve
name called, to which the direction and control of the management o
the affairs of the society is "entrusted to." Thus, the Board is
representative body, a delegatee to whom the management of the affair
of the society is entrusted to by the society, which is none else tha
the general body of the society. Going by the words "entrust", it i
clear that a Board does not have an inherent power to do somethin
which is not entrusted to the Board and it looks upon to the Principal
viz., the general body, for drawing up of an authority to do
particular act. Thus the status of the Board is no more than in th
nature of a delegatee/trustee of an authority given to it by th
society, which is a collective body of the members. Learned Additiona
Advocate General referred to the decision of the Madhya Pradesh Hig
Court reported in AIR 1961 MP 40 (Mishrimal Vs. District Cooperativ
Grower's Association Ltd.). The decision, though, was overruled, in th
decision reported in AIR 1969 SC 1320 (Deccan Merchants Co-operativ
Bank Ltd. v. M/s. Dalichand Jugraj Jain.) on a different aspect, th
observation of the High Court in paragraph 19 on the aspect o
delegation, bears some relevance to the issue raised herein. Th
Madhya Pradesh High Court pointed out:
" A member of the cooperative society is a
shareholder in the society, contributing to the
capital of the society as per the byelaws. The
byelaws produced before us gives the pattern of
shareholders in each of the petitioner cooperative
societies. Thus, the capital of the society is the
sum total of the shares held by the members.
Section 28 touches on the "restriction on or of
share or interest, Section 29 on withdrawal of
shares or interest, Section 30 on transfer of share
or interest on death of members. Section 42 deals
with charge and set off in respect of share or
interest of members on the deposits, bonus payable
to a member/past member, the estate of the deceased
member in respect of any debt due for such member
to the society and set off any sum credited or
payable to a new member towaards payment of any
such debt. Section 44 projects the shares/interest
of a member in the capital of the registered
society or the amount to the credit of a member in
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

the thrift deposit held by the society from
attachment or sale under any decree or order of
Court in respect of any liability incurred by such
member and neither the Official Assignee under the
Presidency-Towns Insolvency Act, 1909 nor a
Receiver under the Provincial Insolvency Act, 1920
shall be entitled to or have any claim on such
share, interest, amount or sum. "
179. Section 72(2) deals with payment of dividend on the shares t
the members from out of the balance net profit after meeting othe
heads under Section 86(1) at the rate not more than 14% per annum o
the paid-up value of each share. The Rules provide for valuation o
shares and the interest in the capital for the purpose of repayment
Thus, going by Section 39, a cooperative society, though not governe
by the Companies Act, nevertheless, is a body corporate and th
interest of the society is the interest of the members collectively
In the circumstances, the law declared by this Court in AIR 1953 Ma
520 (B.N.Viswanathan And Anr. vs Tiffin'S Baryt Asbestos), which ha
stood the test of time on the authority of the general body in th
absence of a Board to appoint a Director, applies to a situatio
herein, that the amendment introduced to restore the authority on th
general body in the absence of the Board to appoint new members, is
reiteration of a legal principal statutorily and hence, well within th
legislative competence of the State and hence, constitutionally valid
We reject the arguments of Mr.V.Raghavachari, learned counsel, in thi
regard. Thus, the contentions raised by the petitioners herein, othe
than the petitioners in W.P.No.34058 of 2012, stand rejected.
180. Learned counsel appearing for the petitioners uniforml
attacked the present amendment, that it is contrary to the decision o
the Full Bench reported in 2006 (1) CTC 1 (K.Nithyanandam Vs. State o
Tamil Nadu). We have already noted that the decision of the Full Benc
is more on the authority of the Special Officer to admit new members
In considering the same, this Court, however, pointed out to th
ultimate authority of the general body and in the context of Section 2
(2) as it stood then, pointed out to the authority of the Board t
admit members. Thus the decision of this Court has to be seen in th
context of the issue raised and the provisions governing the issue. I
must also be noted herein that as far as the decision reported i
(1997) 3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj V. M.L.Nagaraj) is concerned, there is n
provision in the Karnataka Act like Section 21 on the admission o
members by the Board, as it originally stood. Under the Karnataka Act
the Section deals with admission of members by the Society. A readin
of the judgment of the learned single Judge of the Karnataka High Cour
shows that Bye law 16 alone provided for the authority of the Board t
admit new members. So too the Kerala Cooperative Societies Act
Hence, it is difficult to accept the plea of the petitioners that th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

above decisions govern the issue on hand on the constitutionality o
the amended provision.
181. Learned counsel appearing for the petitioners voiced thei
apprehension that the power would be exercised in an unrealistic manne
and against the interests of the society and hence, the amende
provision is liable to be struck down. We do not think that this ca
be a ground for striking down the provision. The parameters laid dow
by the Supreme Court in a series of decisions on the considerations o
the question of constitutionality, clearly, are against the contention
of the petitioners.
182. The petitioners questioned the wisdom of the State in makin
a hurried amendment, particularly in the context of the 97
Constitutional amendment enjoining on the State to hold the election t
the governing body and that the amendment would only undermine th
democratic control, which is the characteristic of the cooperativ
society. We do not think, such argument merits acceptance by thi
Court, in the wake of the observation of the Apex Court in the decisio
reported in AIR 1959 SC 860 (Sardar Sarup Singh and others v. State o
Punjab and others) and AIR 1953 SC 375 K.C. Gajapati Narayan Deo an
Ors. v. State of Orissa, that Courts cannot question the wisdom o
public policy and political wisdom of the State in bringing th
amendment. The satisfaction of the State to issue an Ordinance is als
beyond the pale of interference by this Court – Refer AIR 1974 SC 153
@ 1537 (S.K.G. Sugar Ltd. v. State of Bihar). We may only observ
quoting from the observation of the Supreme Court in the decisio
reported in (1974) 2 SCC 402 (Maganlal Chhagganlal (P) Ltd. V
Municipal Corporation of Greater Bombay & Ors.) that "we should avoi
dogmatic and finical approach when dealing with life's manifol
realities."
183. The case of the writ petitioners in W.P.No.34058 of 2012 i
the case of Joint Farming Societies, however, stands on a differen
footing. Chapter XIII is a specific Chapter relating to Joint Farmin
Societies. Section 94 of the Act is the relevant provision on th
admission of new members. It reads as follows:
" 94. Admission of Members. -- Notwithstanding anything
contained in Section 21 every application for Membership of
a Joint Farming Society shall be considered by the Board
which may grant or refuse admission."
184. Going by the non-obstante clause on the applicability o
Section 21, with no corresponding amendment to Section 89 as in th
line of Section 21(2), on the plain language of Section 94, we agre
with the submission of the learned counsel appearing for the petitione
in W.P.No.34058 of 2012 that the authority for admission of new member
rests only with the Board. Thus, when specific power is given to th
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

Board under a specific provision, the amendment to Section 21 canno
have any effect on the joint farming cooperative societies.
185. Learned counsel appearing for the petitioners in W.P.No.2648
of 2012 pointed out to the admission of 10,000 members in the Sugarcan
Growers' Society, wherein, the total membership prior to the admissio
was only 2000. He pointed out to the hurried manner of admission o
these members, which would only show the non-application of mind an
that such admission would go against the interests of the society i
the conduct of its affairs. Considering the object and the nature o
the society, he pointed out to the impracticability of the admission o
such huge number of persons, which would include ineligible members.
186. If the admission of new members includes ineligible member
and without proper notice, the same can always be corrected unde
Section 20(2)(ii) of the Act by the Registrar, who, on his own motio
or on application, pass orders on the legality of the admission. S
too in the case of writ petition filed as Public Interest Litigation i
W.P.No.27758 of 2012, where the allegation is as to the absence o
proper notice issued and in all cases, where the notice for the genera
body was given by the Special Officer, wherever any such violation o
the provisions of the Act/Rules/byelaws are alleged on the admission o
members, it is open to the aggrieved parties to move for remedia
action before the Registrar. Being an Officer in charge, all that th
Special Officer can do in the given circumstances is to place th
application before the General Body for consideration and for convenin
the general body meeting. In the circumstances, it is not necessary fo
this Court to call for the records regarding the admission of ne
members.
187. Considering the fact that the Registrar has the revisiona
authority, either suo motu or on application where ever there ar
excesses or abuse of the procedure, the Registrar has to take action t
set things right. It may be noted further that on the contention o
the petitioners that all the societies have full membership capacity
learned Advocate General produced before this Court, the details of th
existing membership and that none of the petitioner societies has ful
membership. Thus, admission of new members in a society which does no
have full capacity, cannot be questioned. On going through the same
in the absence of any further material for the petitioners to disput
the same, we hold that so long as there exists room for admission o
new members, it is always open to the Board/general body in the absenc
of the Board, to admit new members. The difficulty on admission o
new members arises only if and when the petitioner society has reache
its full strength as per the byelaws, where the membership is based o
the capital strength of the society and the share allocation pattern.
188. We have gone through the byelaws of the societies produce
https://hcservices.ecourts.gov.in/hcservices/

before this Court, which clearly reveal the pattern of allocation o
shares and the minimum and the maximum number of shares a member ca
hold. Thus, whenever the admission has crossed the maximum number o
members as fixed by the bye laws, the same would be against the byelaw
of the society. Neither a Board nor a general body in the absence o
the Board, can act against this. Thus, it is a matter for th
Registrar to check and act thereon.
189. In the circumstances, we reject the prayer of th
petitioners. We hold that the amendment is constitutionally valid an
intra vires the provisions of the Constitution under Part IX – B of th
Constitution of India, not violative of Article 19(1)(c) to attrac
Article 19(4) and Article 14 of the Constitution of India.
190. We hold that the amendment does not, in any manner, undermin
the bye laws of the society, nor does it interfere with the free wil
and democratic control of the society. Hence, the amendment does no
go against the law laid down in the decisions reported in AIR 1971 S
966 (Damyanti Naranga v. Union of India); (2005) 5 SCC 632 (Zoroastria
Co-operative Housing Society Ltd. V. District Registrar, Co-operativ
(Urban) and Others); (1997) 3 SCC 37 (K.Shantharaj V. M.L.Nagaraj
(2000) 6 SCC 127 (Joint Registrar of Co-operative Societies, Kerala V
T.A.Kuttappan) and 2006 (1) CTC 1 (K.Nithyanandam Vs. State of Tami
Nadu). The amendment brought to the Rules are in consonance with th
amendment to Section 21(2) of the Act. Even if there is any repugnanc
at the time when the meeting was called for, the Section cannot, o
that account, be held as bad.
In the circumstances, barring W.P.No.34058 of 2012 all the othe
writ petitions are dismissed and W.P.No.34058 of 2012 is allowed. N
costs. Consequently, connected Miscellaneous Petitions are closed.
 Sd/-
 Asst.Registrar.
 /true copy/
 Sub Asst.Registrar.
